HARVARD UNIVERSITY LIBRARY 

Foreword

What this book is. This book is a combination laboratory manual, study guide, review program, and testing program, with objective tests furnished under separate cover and a complete review unit furnished at the back of this book, illustrated in two colors.

What this book does. This Guide was prepared with two main purposes in view: (1) to help the student to help himself in his study activities and laboratory activities, (2) to help the instructor by relieving him of much of the routine work associated with the making of assignments, special directions, and individual explanations, thus giving each student a fair division of the instructor's time. 

The use of this Guide does not mean more subject matter for the student, nor does it mean more work for the teacher. In fact, it means less work for each. 

The study activities and the laboratory activities can be corrected easily and quickly in class by the instructor and students.

Unit organization. The material is arranged in twelve main units or areas of instruction, each representing an important concept in the field of physics. 

The lesson units. The twelve larger units of work are further divided into lesson units or problems which, insofar as possible, are tied to everyday interests and activities.

Job sheets. Each problem, whether laboratory activity or study activity, can be torn from the book without mutilating any other problem in the book. 

Each sheet is a "job sheet" or "work sheet."

Usable with any text. Specific references to twelve commonly used physics texts are furnished in this Physics Guide. Each of these reference tables immediately precedes the unit of work which it concerns. The material can be taught in the order given; or any high school text can be followed since the corresponding lessons are suitable-regardless of the text used. 

Supplementary activities, review, self-tests, extra practice. At the close of each unit supplementary activities, review questions, and self-tests are provided. These materials furnish activities for all students, regardless of their individual differences. 

The activities at the ends of the different units include the following: (1) Activities That You Will

Enjoy, (2) Individual and Group Projects, (3) Topics for Further Study, (4) Review Outline with Review 

Questions, (5) "How Good Are You at Solving Problems?", (6) "How Well Do You Think?", (7) Selftest. 

Three kinds of tests. (1) Self-tests for review purposes and "How-well-do-you-think?" tests for applying scientific thinking are presented directly in this book; (3) complete objective tests for grading purposes are furnished under separate cover, with a choice of forms.

Inductive presentation. In general, the inductive method of presentation is followed in this Physics 

Guide. Through the use of simple language and familiar illustrations, the student is led to draw his own conclusions in the applications of laws and principles. The questions raised are of such nature as to challenge curiosity and arouse interest. Note the main heading for any activity in this book.

Three-level study plan. The activities are arranged on a three-level study plan, designated as A, B, and C. The first, or A level, deals with those fundamental concepts, which every pupil should know if he is to have an elementary understanding of the subject. This division is supposed to include the "irreducible minimum" required for an elementary course in high school physics. The students who have special interest in the subject, or those who are preparing for college or a technical course in engineering should do the B exercises in an activity in addition to the A exercises. The pupils who have a special aptitude for, and an enthusiastic interest in, the subject should do the C exercises in addition to the A and B exercises. 

A special review unit. At the end of this Physics

Guide is a complete review unit which is offered as a means for a rapid review of any portion of the course, or of the entire course. This review is simplified and illustrated with two-color diagrams.

Welcome to Some New Adventures! 

Adventures in physics. Every scientist is an explorer whose adventures are just as thrilling as those of Columbus or Admiral Byrd. The story of the explorations and problems of physics is a long one. This story has come down to us from such men as Archimedes who proposed to move the world if he could find a support upon which to place his lever, Newton who asked himself why the apple fell from the tree to the ground, Watt who set the genii of his grandmother's teakettle to work, Volta who invented the electric battery, Faraday whose experiments with a coil of wire and a magnet ushered in the age of electric energy, Edison who rubbed the Aladdin lamp of his genius and gave us the incandescent lamp, Roentgen who called an unknown ray the "x ray," the Curies who isolated radium, Einstein who gave us strange and startling theories of matter and energy.

The laws and principles of physics are based upon experimentation, and every experiment is in a sense a question put to Nature. Mother Nature sometimes gives her answer readily, but sometimes she appears to guard her secrets as carefully as a miser guards his gold.

The story of the discoveries made by the many workers in physics, and the applications of these discoveries to the affairs of everyday life is a long one. 

In order to help you find your way in the maze of details, this Guide is written. 

A road sign to study. When you go on an automobile trip to some distant point in an unfamiliar part of the country, you observe carefully the road signs. These signs protect you from dangers of sharp curves and narrow bridges, and also, save your time by directing you to your destination.

This book is, in a sense, a road sign to study. It is designed as a guide to the study of high school physics. It seeks to help you get your lessons with a minimum of effort; it seeks to help you understand more clearly what scientists, the world over, are doing. 

How to use this book. The following general method for using this book is suggested. Your instructor may request you to use a modification of this method. You yourself, however, should determine experimentally how you can get the best results and change your methods accordingly.

First, note the subjects assigned by your teacher, and then read the first few paragraphs of the proper lesson in this guide book. This will give you a bird'seye view of what the important points in the lesson are. 

Second, turn to your text and read the entire lesson covering the subjects assigned. Read with a fair degree of speed, but with concentration. Note especially the meaning of technical terms, laws, and principles. 

Third, you are now ready for the exercises in the

Guide. Write the correct words or numbers on the blank lines. 

As a final step, check your answers by referring to your textbook. Make changes and additions if necessary. If your answers differ from those indicated in the text, try to find the reason. Do not make your exercises a mere matter of copying. This is a waste of time. You may fool yourself, but not the teacher. 

How to do the laboratory activities. Perform each laboratory activity according to the directions given. Learn how to use scientific tools accurately and carefully. Observe the method as well as the results. As a general rule good scientists are good experimenters. The controlled laboratory activities are given to help you learn how to experiment and to give you practice in drawing correct conclusions from a group of related facts. 

In each laboratory activity attempt to get as accurate results as possible with the apparatus available. 

The laboratory is a place to find the truth. Avoid drawing conclusions that go beyond the facts—this statement holds in everyday life as well as in the laboratory for when people avoid drawing conclusions that go beyond the facts at hand, there are fewer cases of malicious gossip and there may be fewer cases of incipient Hitlerism in the world. Some people think a laboratory is a good place to learn to live in a democracy.

The data you record should always be the data you secure. Any attempt to record data you did not get destroys the purpose of the laboratory. 

Checking answers and experimental data. 

Each incorrect answer should be corrected by you.

Your instructor may indicate the correct answer for the entire class as a whole. If you are requested to answer the questions you should also be given the opportunity of knowing how well you answered them. 

But you not your instructor-are responsible for getting the correct answers. 

How to solve problems. Never attempt to solve a problem until you know exactly what it means and what is required.

Whenever possible, write a problem in equational form. A mathematical equation is simply a device for saving time. For example, the expression for the circumference of a circle can be written in mathematical shorthand as: C=2πr.

In making mathematical computations, always look for short cuts. Suppose, for example, that you wish to find the ratio of the volume of a sphere of radius 9 cm to its surface area. The following is the long and tedious way of solving the problem: 

V=3r³×3.1416x93-3053.6352 cm³. 

A=4πr²=4×3.1416×92=1017.8784 cm².

Hence, =3053.6352/1017.8784-3.

The problem can be solved much more easily as follows: 

γ 9 

A 4πr2 3 3 

= =3. 

The alert student is always on the lookout for short cuts. vii

1. How Can You Measure Accurately to the Nearest 

Tenth of a Centimeter?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To learn the metric units for measuring length, how to measure length, and the number of centimeters in one inch.

Apparatus. Meter stick with metric scale on one edge and English scale on the other edge-Labora tory table. 

How to get ready for this activity. You are familiar with the common English units employed in measuring length, such as the inch, foot, and mile. 

The scientist uses the metric system for measuring length and other quantities. The first four activities in this book indicate why the metric system is used in science.

In the metric system the fundamental unit of length is the meter, which is very nearly one tenmillionth part of the distance between the equator and the north pole of the earth on the meridian of Paris. The meter is slightly longer than 1 yard. For measuring small distances the meter has been divided into smaller parts called decimeters (dm), centimeters (cm), and millimeters (mm). The relations are: 

10 mm = 1 cm, 10 cm = 1 dm, 10 dm = 1 meter. Most measurements in the laboratory are made in cm. 

For measuring large distances, the kilometer (km) = 1,000 meters is used. To change from one unit to another in this system, you merely multiply or divide by a multiple of 10. Because of its convenience this system is used by scientists all over the world. 

In this activity you measure the length of a labortable atory At first this appears to be a rather top. useless task. To know the length of a certain table top to an accuracy of 1/10 in. probably has no practical value. But it is important to learn how to measure accurately within the limits of the apparatus used.

E' (2) If the ends of the meter stick are defaced, the scale readings should begin at some distance from the end. It is convenient to start with the initial reading at the 1-cm mark or at the 10-cm mark. This reading must be subtracted from the reading at the other end of the meter stick. (3) In making measurements, do not scratch or mar the table in any way. 

To avoid this, a sheet of paper should be placed on the table under the end of the meter stick, and held in place by means of a weight. Make end marks on the paper, and not on the table. (4) Avoid errors in reading due to the position of the eye with reference to the scale. If the meter stick is placed on its flat side, an error is likely to be made in the reading, as shown in Figure 1. When the eye is at E', the position of the object appears to have shifted. The apparent shifting of the object due to a change in position of the eye of the observer, is called parallax. 

To avoid errors due to parallax, the meter stick should be placed on edge. (5) Several measurements must be taken at different places. This is necessary, first, because the dimensions of the object being measured are not the same at different places; and second, because the person doing the measuring cannot be sure that he has the reading just right.

What to do. Measure the length of the table top in five different places in cm and tenths of a cm, and find the average as your best determination of the length.

Measure the length in inches the same number of times. If the inch scale is divided into eighths, express eighths as tenths. Find the average of these measurements. 

Calculate the number of cm in 1 in. by dividing the average length in cm by the average length in inches. 

From the known value of the number of cm in 

1 in., find the error and per cent error in your measurement.

2. How Can You Measure Accurately to the Nearest 

Hundredth of a Centimeter?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To learn to use a vernier caliper.

Apparatus. Vernier caliper-Cylindrical cup— Graduate Glass plate. 

How to get ready for this activity. In measurements made by a meter stick, the readings are accurate only to the nearest millimeter. Fractional parts of a millimeter must be estimated. Many things must be measured with greater accuracy than this. 

For example, in a machine shop the diameters of cylinders, bolts, and other machine parts must meet exact specifications.

The vernier caliper is a device for measuring fractional parts. For example, the millimeter divisions on the metric scale can be measured to tenths. Figure 

3 shows a vernier caliper, which has a fixed scale and a sliding scale (vernier scale). Ten divisions on the vernier scale correspond to 9 mm on the fixed scale, or 1 division on the vernier scale corresponds to 0.9 mm on the fixed scale. If the jaws are closed, the zero marks on the two scales coincide. If the jaws are now opened 0.1 mm, the first line on the vernier scale coincides with the first millimeter line on the fixed scale; if the jaws are opened 0.2 mm, the second line on the vernier scale coincides with the second millimeter line on the fixed scale; if the jaws are opened 0.7 mm, the seventh line on the vernier scale coincides with the seventh millimeter line on the fixed scale, etc.

MW.MXZ 

Figure 4 gives an example in the method of reading a vernier. The distance from A to B is 1.3 cm. 

To determine the fractional part of a mm from B to C, note that line 7 on the vernier most nearly coincides with a line on the fixed scale. Therefore the distance from B to C=0.7 mm =0.07 cm. Hence, the distance from A to C=1.37 cm.

The caliper can be used to measure the inside diameter of a cup by using the jaws on the other side of the caliper. Pull these jaws apart until they fit against the inside walls of the cup. 

The depth of a vessel can be measured by using the sliding bar on the side of the "handle" of the vernier caliper.

What To Do. Measure the inside diameter of a cylindrical cup in five different places, and take the average as your best result. Measure the inside depth of the cup in five different places, and find the average depth. Compute the volume in cubic centimeters (cc) using the average value of each dimension. The formula for the volume of a cylindrical cup is: V = r²h In this formula r is the radius, and h is the depth of the cup.

Fill the cup with as much water as it will hold, then place the glass plate on the top and push off the excess water until the cup is just level full with no air bubbles below the plate. Pour the water in the graduate and read the volume of the water, and, hence, the volume of the cup.

Figure 3 

Suppose that you wish to measure the outside diameter of a cylinder. Place the cylinder between the jaws of the caliper and push in the movable jaw until the two jaws fit snugly against the cylinder. 

The position of the zero mark on the vernier scale is read to the whole number of centimeters and millimeters. The line on the vernier scale that coincides with some line of the fixed scale is then read. 

The number of this millimeter line is the last figure in the reading. Thus, the length is determined to tenths of a millimeter, or hundredths of a centimeter. 

CALIPER SCALE

11. Why is the volume as determined by the vernier caliper so much more accurate than the volume as determined by a meter stick?

Which, if either, would yield the more accurate volume? Why?

3. How Can You Measure Accurately to the Nearest 

Thousandth of a Centimeter?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To learn to use a micrometer caliper. 

Apparatus. Micrometer caliper-Several pieces of wire of known gauge numbers. 20 How to get ready for this activity. The men and women of today who help to make our airplanes, our automobiles, and our industrial machines know that many measurements have a "tolerance" of only 

2 thousandths of an inch or less. How can you detect such small distances?

In this class you may get practice in precise measuring. For example, in setting up electrical apparatus, one is frequently instructed to use a wire of a given diameter, which is usually designated by a certain gauge number, as for example, No. 30.

Since the diameter of a wire is so small, a measurement of a wire must be made with greater accuracy than can be obtained with the vernier caliper. Tables usually express the diameter of a wire in millimeters, or in thousandths of an inch, or mils. If expressed in metric units, the diameter is given to hundredths of a millimeter, that is, to thousandths of a centi- 'meter. The diameter of a No. 17 wire is 1.15 mm. 

Another example in which measurements must be made with an accuracy within a few thousandths of a millimeter or inch occurs in the automobile. A rod must be made to fit a certain hole, and the diameter must be checked by some device that can measure with the accuracy mentioned above. 

When the jaws touch, the zero mark on the horizontal scale C and the zero mark on the circular scale D should coincide. If these two zero marks do not coincide, the difference in scale reading must be added to or subtracted from the final reading. Whether we add or subtract depends upon whether the zero mark on the circular scale D runs by, or does not come quite up to the zero mark on the horizontal scale C. 

Suppose that the scale C is divided into millimeters, and that when E makes one revolution, it moves forward or backward one division on scale C, that is, 1 mm. If D has 100 divisions, each division represents 1/100 mm, or 1/1,000 cm. On some in-. struments D has but 50 divisions, but 2 revolutions are necessary to move E forward or backward 1 mm.

In this case C has half-millimeter divisions. 

In order to measure the thickness of an object with the micrometer caliper, the object is placed between the jaws and the milled head turned until the jaws just make contact with the object. The whole number of millimeters is read by the position of the edge of scale D on scale C. The number of the division mark on scale D that coincides with the index line on scale C gives the number of hundredths of a millimeter.

What To Do. Measure the diameter of each of the wires five times in different positions and take the average as your best result. Check your value of each diameter with the value for that gauge number as given in the Reference Tables in this book. 

Find your error and per cent error. 

Practice measuring other objects, such as the diameter of a pencil, the diameter of a pin, the diameter of a marble or steel ball, the diameter of a hair.

Figure 5

A device used for making these accurate measurements is the micrometer caliper (Figure 5). This caliper is like the vernier caliper in that a movable scale D moves on a fixed scale C. A complete revolution of the milled head E causes the scale D to move forward or backward over one of the least divisions of the scale C.

In order to take readings with the micrometer caliper, it is necessary, first, to determine the zero reading. To do this the milled head E is turned forward until the jaws at B just come into contact. 

The milled head should never be turned with very great force. It should be held lightly between the finger and thumb so that when the jaws come together, the fingers will slip. Some instruments are provided with a ratchet head, which makes this precaution unnecessary.

4. How Could You Weigh Accurately the Balance Wheel of Your Watch?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To learn to weigh things accurately on a beam balance; to find the density of a material. 

Apparatus. Beam balance-Set of weights-Ver nier caliper Cylindrical cup-Glass plate-Metal cylinder. 

How to get ready for this activity. Would you weigh the balance wheel of your watch in ounces? 

The scientist would use the metric system. In the metric system small things are weighed in grams or fractional parts of a gram. A gram weight is the weight of 1 cc of pure water at a temperature of 4° C. 

A set of metric weights is shown in Figure 6. 

As soon as you are through with any weight, put it back in its compartment in the weight box. 

What is mass? The mass of an object is a measure of the quantity of material in it. Mass may be determined by using balance scales. The mass of an object is always the same; its weight may vary. 

For example, you weigh less on a mountain or in a flying airplane than you do on the ground. The farther an object gets above the surface of the earth, the less it weighs; its mass, however, remains the same.

Ts 100 50 

Figure 6

You will be using the beam balance to weigh different objects. Figure 8 shows a trip balance, and 

Figure 7 shows a more accurate beam balance. In each balance the pointer moves over a scale when the beam or "cross bar" swings. You can turn the small nut (S in Figure 8) to put the balance in equilibrium, making the pointer rest at zero on the scale. شاد 

Figure 8 

What to do to find the mass of water. Find the mass of the cylindrical cup and glass plate together. Fill the cup with water and cover with the glass plate, being careful that the cup is just full and contains no large air bubbles; weigh again to find the mass of the cup, plate, and water. From these data determine the mass of the water in the cup. 

As a check on this method find the volume of the cup by measuring the diameter and depth with the vernier caliper. Find the mass of the water from the relation of the number of grams in 1 cc of water. 

MASS OF WATER 

Figure 7

To weigh an object proceed as follows: Move the rider R to the left end of the beam, and adjust the balancing nut until the pointer, when at rest, is at the zero of the scale. Place the object to be weighed in the left-hand pan. Add weights gently to the right-hand pan, beginning with a large weight and continuing with smaller weights until the balance is almost in equilibrium. With most balances of this type it is not necessary to use weights from the weight box that have a value less than 10 gm. Moving the rider R all the way to the right is equivalent to adding 10 gm to the right pan. The scale on which 

R moves is divided into grams and tenths of a gram. 

Secure the final adjustment for equilibrium by adjusting R. Find the total of all the weights in the right pan plus the reading on the beam.

5. One Dam Holding Twice as Much Water as Another May Not Need Any More Concrete in Its Construction. Why? 

The construction of Boulder Dam across the Colorado River is regarded as an outstanding engineering feat. It contains 6,600,000 tons of concrete. Its sloping walls are 660 feet thick at the bottom and 45 feet thick at the top. It is designed to hold back a lake of water 115 miles long. If the lake extended back only one tenth as far, would the dam need be made but one tenth as strong?

The pressure at any point of a dam is equal to the depth times the density (p=hd). The total force on the surface of a dam is equal to the pressure times the area (F=pA). 

29. Therefore, the force of the water on a dam is equal to the depth times the density times the area:

F=hdA in which h is the average height (or depth) of the water, that is, one half the vertical distance from the surface to the bottom.

6. How Much Water Do You Use Each Month; How Are You Able to Get This Much Water? 

People in the country dig wells for water or catch it in the rainy season and store it in cisterns. People in the city turn a faucet. What an easy way to get water!

Before water can run so easily from a faucet, work must be done to install pipes, and pressure must be applied to force the water through the pipes. Usually it is necessary to pump the water from wells, lakes, or rivers into water towers or pressure tanks.

7. How Can You Stop a Heavy Automobile by Applying a Small Force With Your Foot? 

If the driver of an automobile keeps his car under control, he must be able to stop within a reasonable distance and not swerve to either side of the road. 

The modern automobile is equipped with hydraulic brakes. With these brakes a small force applied to the brake pedal is multiplied many times to produce a large force on the wheels. Fortunately, this force can be distributed to all the wheels in a manner which reduces the tendency to swerve. 

How does a barber's chair operate? How do hydraulic automobile brakes operate? How can you use a liquid to multiply force?

Topics to read about. Pascal's law-Relation between areas and forces when force is transmitted through a liquid-Relation between areas of pistons and their displacements-Hydraulic press-Hydraulic brakes. 

Force from your foot must get to the wheels of your car. How? You can easily break a strong jug filled with a liquid merely by pushing hard on the stopper. 

1. In a confined liquid, pressure applied at any given point is transmitted equally in directions and without loss. 

2. This means that if a force of 10 lb. is applied to 1 in.2 of a confined liquid, the force exerted upon each in.2 of the surface of the containing vessel is lb.

Why do your hydraulic brakes push harder on the wheels than your foot pushes on the pedal? Consider Figure 16 which is a cross-sectional view of Pascal's apparatus. Suppose that the area of the lower side of the small piston P is 1 in.2 and that of the large piston P₁ is 100 in.2 1 

3. When 1 lb. is applied to P, what upward force is exerted on P₁? 

4. If P₁ is pushed down with a force of 100 lb., what upward force will be exerted on P? 

When you push on the pedal of hydraulic brakes, the force is transmitted through a liquid to the brakes. The pedal is attached to a "master piston" which pushes the liquid toward four brake pistons, one attached to each brake. 

5. If the brake pedal supplies a force of 1 lb. to each in. of the "master piston" (attached to the pedal), how much force is applied to each in.2 of each of the four pistons at the wheels? 

Suppose that each of the four brake pistons has the same area as the master pedal piston. 

6. If a force of 10 lb. is applied to the master piston, how much force is applied to the brake rods? 

Hydraulic brakes gain force. What do they lose? A small force applied to the long arm of a lever can overcome a large force on the short arm of the lever. In the hydraulic brake there must be a similar compensation for the gain in force. The area of P is 1 in.2 and that of P₁ is 100 in.2 

7. When P is pushed down 1 in., how far does P₁ move up? 

8. When P₁ is pushed down 10 in., how many inches will P move up, provided the small tube is long enough? 

9. Whenever force is gained in a hydraulic brake system, is lost. 

How much force can you gain by using hydraulic pressure? Assume that the diameter of the small piston in Figure 16 is 1 in. and that of the large piston is 12 in. If a force of 100 lb. is applied to the small piston, what upward force is exerted on the large piston? To solve this problem you will have to calculate the areas of the ends of the two pistons. These areas can be calculated by using the formula for the area of a circle, A = πr².

8. You Can Distinguish Many Substances by Finding Their Different Densities. 

How Can You Determine Density? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To find the densities of various solids and liquids.

Apparatus. Beam balance-Set of weights- 

Large beaker-Graduate-Block of wood-Small metal cylinder-Kerosene-Carbon tetrachloride or salt water-Irregularly shaped objects-Overflow can. 

How to get ready for this activity. If you try to lift a block of wood and a block of iron that have the same volume, you note that the iron is much heavier than the wood; iron has a much greater mass for the same volume. In describing a material, the scientist often lists as one property the mass per unit volume. This is called the density, which in metric units is usually expressed in gm per cc. 

To determine the density of a material, we measure the mass and volume of an object made of the material. The mass is easily determined by weighing. If the object is a solid with a regular shape, its dimensions can be measured readily and the volume calculated. But, if the object is a solid irregular in shape, or is a liquid, how can its volume be determined? 

How to find the density of a wooden block or any other regular solid. Determine the mass by a beam balance, and the volume by the method indicated. 

Measure each dimension with the vernier caliper in three different places and take the average as your best result.

9. Without Damaging It, How Can You Tell Whether a Spoon Is Solid Silver or Only Silver Plated?

According to story, Hiero, King of Syracuse, suspected that a crown made by his goldsmith was adulterated with silver. He asked Archimedes to determine whether the crown was pure gold. Archimedes worked long and hard upon the problem. One day while in one of the baths of his native city, he noted the buoyant effect of the water upon his body and he thought of a method of solving his problem. 

What was his solution? 

How can you tell whether a silver spoon is solid silver or silver plated? What is density? What is the density of gold? of silver? How can you determine the density of a spoon or the density of a crown?

Topics to read about. Relation between specific gravity and density-Specific gravity and density of a solid (some textbooks use specific weight to mean specific gravity)-Archimedes' principle. 

Any cc of solid, pure silver weighs 10.5 grams. 

If you can find the volume of a spoon, you can find what it ought to weigh in order to be made of pure silver. This is the same as saying that the density of pure silver is 10.5 gm per cc. The term density represents the mass of a unit volume of a material. 

The density of pure silver is always 10.5 gm per cc. 

If you have exactly 2.5 cc of silver, you can compute exactly how much it weighs without actually weighing it. All you have to do is multiply the density of silver by the number of cubic centimeters of silver that you have.

Density can be found mathematically as- Mass

Density=Volume 

Density is always a concrete number. It can be expressed as gm per cc, lb. per ft.³, or any unit of mass per any unit of volume.

Specific gravity compares the weight of a substance with the weight of an equal volume of water. Specific gravity gives the answer to the question: "How many times as heavy as water is this material, when we compare equal volumes?" 

Weight of object

Specific gravity=Weight of equal volume of water Specific gravity compares weights. Since it compares like quantities, it is an abstract number. If the mass of a body is measured in gm and the volume in cc, the density and specific gravity are numerically equal.

If a metal is attached to wood, the wood can be made to sink in water, showing that the mass per unit volume of the combination is greater than that of wood. If a light metal is mixed with a heavy metal, the mixture will have a smaller mass per unit volume than the heavy metal has.

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To find the relation between the density of a liquid and its lifting force, or buoyancy. 

Apparatus. Beam balance-Large beaker-Small stone or small metal cylinder-Block of wood-Kerosene-Carbon tetrachloride--Overflow can. 

How to get ready for this activity. If you have tried to lift a stone while it is submerged, you probably have noticed that when it is under water, it appears to be lighter than when it is above water. 

Evidently, the water exerts an upward force on the stone. Why does rock sink and wood float? Would a stone sink in mercury?

6-10- How to find the buoyancy on an 'immersed object. When a stone is immersed, how does its apparent loss of weight compare with the weight of the liquid that the stone displaces?

Weigh a small stone (or a metal cylinder) in air; then find its apparent weight in water by suspending it from the balance as shown in Figure 21. 

Pour water into the overflow can until the water just reaches the spout. Then lower the stone gently into the overflow can. The volume of the water that flows into the beaker is equal to the volume of the stone. Weigh the beaker and the overflow water, and determine the weight of the water. This is the weight of the water displaced by the stone.

How to find the buoyancy on a floating object. 

If an object floats on a liquid, it apparently loses all its weight. However, an object floats at different depths in different liquids; that is, the volume of the displaced liquid is not the same in one liquid as it is in another liquid having a different density. 

Select a block of wood and check to see that it has been treated with paraffin to prevent the absorption of water. Weigh the wood in air. Place it in an overflow can, which is filled to the spout with water. 

Catch the overflow water and determine its weight. 

Compare the weight of the block with that of the displaced water. Pour the water into a graduate and determine its volume. 

Repeat the experiment using kerosene as the liquid.

Repeat the experiment again using carbon tetrachloride.

Is a candle made of paraffin or tallow? Weigh a candle to determine its mass; then find its volume by the water displacement method. This can be done quite accurately by using an overflow can. If you do not have an overflow can, fill a graduate partly full of water and read the volume; then place the candle into the water and read the volume again. 

The increase in volume is the volume of the candle. 

The method just described is less accurate than the overflow method.

Figure 24

The hydrometer rod in Figure 24 has a crosssectional area of 1 cm² and is weighted at one end to make it float upright. When it is placed in water, it sinks just far enough to displace a weight of water equal to its own weight. The lighter the liquid, the farther the hydrometer sinks, or, to be more scientific, the less the density of a liquid, the farther a hydrometer sinks. 

Since the cross-sectional area of your "homemade" hydrometer is 1 cm², the reading of the instrument, when placed in a liquid, gives directly the volume of the liquid displaced. Use the hydrometer rod in water to find the volume V₁ of the water which the rod displaces. Then find the volume V2 of the copper sulfate solution which the rod displaces. 

If d represents the density of the liquid and v its volume, the mass of the displaced water=d₁₁ and the mass of the copper sulfate solution=d2v2. 

Since the density of water=1 gm per cc, d₂=v1/v2. 

32. Specific gravity of the copper sulfate solution Your automobile battery contains dilute sulfuric acid. When your battery is discharged, the solution has a specific gravity of 1.1. When the battery is fully charged, the specific gravity is 1.3. (The specific gravity varies slightly with different batteries.)

My Solution 

What is one method of determining the answer to: "What is it made of?" -30-

1. Make a small model of a hydraulic press. The large cylinder can be made from a piece of smooth brass tubing 6 or 8 in. long and 3 in. or more in diameter. The small cylinder may consist of a tube a half in. in diameter. The small tube should be inserted near the bottom of the large cylinder and then bent upward, somewhat as shown in Figure 16. The pistons should be machine turned so as to fit the cylinders accurately. Itisnot absolutely necessary that valves be inserted since the motions of the pistons and the loads, which they will bear, can be illustrated by exerting force on either piston. If a piece of rubber hose 4 or 5 ft. long is attached to the small cylinder, the pressure on the piston can be supplied by pouring water into the hose. This gives a very good demonstration of Pascal's tube and cask experiment. 

2. Examine the operation of an automobile hydraulic jack. Note the lever and piston systems of the jack.

Find out how many strokes of the lever are required to lift a given weight 6 in. Compute how many times the force F is multiplied. 

3. Read the water meter in your home. On the day on which the official watermeter reader appears, make a record of the reading of the meter dials. At the end of the month make a second reading. The difference between these two readings gives the amount of water consumed. Check your values with those reported by the water company.

13. Why do Same People Get an Earache When 

They Go Up in an Airplane?

Long ago men became curious about air. They wondered if it were "real" or if it had weight.

They wondered what it was made of. Today we know that air is a mixture of a number of different gases, the most plentiful of which are nitrogen and oxygen. Oxygen enables you to breathe and live and to have fire. Nitrogen helps to build your tissues and the tissues of other living things. 

Not many years ago few people knew that there is a region known as the stratosphere. In 1931 

Piccard made the first balloon ascent into this region which begins at an altitude of approximately 10 miles. Between the surface of the earth and the stratosphere lies the troposphere, the storm region of our atmosphere. In any part of the stratosphere or troposphere that has been investigated it has been found that nearly one fifth of the atmosphere is oxygen, and nearly four fifths of it is nitrogen, together with small quantities of other gases. 

How much air pressure is on your body? How is air pressure measured? How does air pressure vary as you go up in an airplane?.. What causes air pressure?

Topics to read about. The atmosphere, its composition and zones-Atmospheric pressure; the pressure of "one atmosphere"-Barometers and their uses; altimeters.

14. Why Do People Who Climb Pike's Peak Have Difficulty in Breathing Near the Top of the Mountain? 

Near Colorado Springs is Pike's Peak, a mountain peak that has fascinated generations of people by its picturesqueness and its height. Each year many nature lovers who can stand the physical exertion, hike to the top of this mountain to see the extraordinary sunrises and to enjoy the thrill of having conquered a difficult climb. Near the top many people must stop to rest every few feet. Near the top of any high mountain most people must walk slowly. Why? 

How much would the air in your laboratory weigh if the laboratory were on top of Pike's Peak? Why is the air pressure on top of Pike's Peak less than the air in pressure your laboratory? Why do you breathe? Why do people breathe rapidly on Pike's Peak?

How much would the air in your laboratory weigh if the laboratory were on the top of 

Pike's Peak? Gravity pulls down on all parts of the air. Hence, any layer of air has to support all the air above it. As you go higher in the atmosphere there is less and less material to be supported. 

Air, like all gases, can be compressed by applying force to it; the greater the compressing force, the greater the density. Air at a high altitude supports less weight than air at a low altitude does. This means that at high altitudes the density is less and the air is thinner than at low altitudes.

As a result, when you stand on the top of Pike's Peak which is about 21½ miles high, approximately half the mass of our atmosphere is beneath you. 

When you fly at a height of seven miles in an airplane, approximately three-fourths of the mass of the atmosphere is beneath you. 

10. Air, which is compressed, has a greater than air not compressed. 

11. The air at the surface of the earth has a greater than air at high altitudes. 

12. The greater the density, the the pull of gravity on any given volume of air.

15. How Can You Use a Barometer to Predict the Weather? "A clear sky at night is the sailor's delight.' "The sun set red today, so I'm sure the weather will change." Have you ever heard any of these old statements? Weather reporting used to be mostly guesswork; today it is a science. 

A scientific forecaster is able to predict the weather conditions a few days in advance, thus informing the aviator when or when not to fly, enabling the farmer to know when to cut his hay, and helping you to determine whether to start on a trip. How is it possible to make scientific predictions about anything as fickle as the weather? 

Which is heavier, a cubic foot of dry air, or a cubic foot of air saturated with water vapor? How can you predict when it is going to rain or when it is going to be fair and clear?

17. How Can You "Pump" Air or Water

Whenever the speed of a fluid increases, pressure decreases. If this fact were not true, you would not have the modern radio or the modern electric lights that you now use. Bernoulli's principle enables us to "pump" the air from radio tubes and light tubes.

This increases the speed of molecules through the jet and decreases the pressure around the jet. The same principle is used in mixing air and gasoline vapor in the carburetor of an automobile.

18. How Can You Make Water Run Faster Through One Siphon 

Than It Does Through Another Siphon?

Purpose. To discover how the size of the tube, the difference in level, and the nature of the liquid affect the rate of flow of a liquid through a siphon. 

Apparatus. Glass or rubber tubes for siphons-

Factor A=Diameter of tube Factor B Difference in level of liquid

The class may be divided into three groups, each group finding the effect of one factor on the rate of flow, while the other factors are controlled. 

Group I: vary factor A; control factors B and C. 

Group II: vary factor B; control factors A and C. 

Group III: vary factor C; control factors A and B.

What to do. Fill a large, glass beaker or jar with water. Place another large beaker at the same level as the first beaker. Fill a glass or rubber tube with water to remove the air within it. distance from the highest point of the siphon to the level of the liquid in the higher container measured vertically.

The length of the long arm is the distance, measured vertically, from the highest point to the open end of the tube, or to the water level in the lower container if the "open end" is submerged.

The air pressure on the water in the higher container can be considered equal to the air pressure on the water in the lower container because the differences in level are so small.

The two forces causing water to flow through a siphon are air pressure and the weight of the water in the vertical arms of the siphon. 

Group I: How is the rate of flow through a siphon affected by the diameter of the tube?

Use tubes of different diameter; control the difference in levels. Use the same liquid.

To maintain a constant level, pour more water into the top container or raise the container, thus keeping the top of the liquid at a fixed level. 

To measure the quantity of liquid that flows through the siphon in unit time, two procedures can be used. Measure the time required to fill a container with water, or control the time in each case and measure the volume of liquid that flows. 

A table of results should include the average of several trials for each tube used.

In determining the effect of the diameter of the tube on the rate of flow, it should be possible to measure the internal diameter of the tubes with reasonable accuracy. This will be true if fairly large tubes are used.

How can compressed air be used to stop a train, or operate riveting hammers, rock drills, and stonecutting tools?

20. Why Is Tom Jones Sick? 

CONTROLLED LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Yesterday Tom Jones walked under a ladder. 

Today he is sick. Tom Jones also ate 4 bars of candy, drank 5 bottles of pop, and had less sleep than he usually does. We think that Tom is joking, but he says that walking under the ladder made him ill.

When you try to detect the causes of something, often find that there is not one cause but 2 you or 3 or even more causes-and each influence may the others. Tom ate too much candy; he drank too much pop; he had too little sleep. His illness may be due to three "causes." 

In everyday life people often make the mistake of looking for a simple, single cause of something. "A Townsend law would have given us prosperity,' but "prosperity" is not a simple thing and has no simple cause. Like most things it has several causes or variable factors.

There is a way of determining how the different "causes" or variable factors are related: through a controlled experiment. This is the type of experiment that scientists try to use. It is the type that Dr. Walter Reed used when he found that malaria is caused by mosquitoes. Dr. Reed, using volunteers, put a number of men in a shed. All the men lived under the same conditions, except one condition. They ate the same food cooked in the same kettle; they slept in the same air; they drank from the same water. There was one condition that was different; mosquitoes were allowed to bite half of the men but not the other half. Several of the men bitten by the mosquitoes became ill with malaria; none of the unbitten men became ill. The world then knew that the bite of a certain kind of mosquito may cause malaria.

In a controlled experiment certain factors are kept constant. By a controlled experiment it is possible to determine how one factor depends upon any other factor.

In the following laboratory exercise you can try your hand at performing a controlled experiment.

HOW DOES THE VOLUME OF A GAS CHANGE WHEN PRESSURE IS APPLIED? 

Purpose. To determine the relation between the pressure and volume of a given mass of gas, if the temperature is constant.

Apparatus. Glass tube bent in the form of a J with the short arm closed-Mercury-Meter stick -Barometer. 

How to get ready for this activity. If pressure is applied to a toy balloon filled with air, the volume changes. As air is pumped into a bicycle tire, the pump becomes warm. Some of the heat is produced by compressing the air; the compression of any gas normally produces heat as well as a change in volume. 

What to do. Pour enough mercury into the J tube to fill the curved part of it. Shake the tube until the levels are the same. The air in the tube 

AB is enclosed. This is the volume of air on which pressure is to be added. The length AB can be considered equivalent to the volume of the air in AB since the inside diameter of the tube is uniform. 

If the level of the mercury in the closed side of the tube is the same as the level of the mercury in the open side, the pressure on the air that is enclosed is equal to the pressure of the atmosphere at the open end of the tube. The barometer reading will give the pressure of the air at the open end of the tube.

1. Make a jet pump. This can be done by using glass tubes of different diameters, somewhat as shown in Figure 26. If glass is used, it will be necessary for the operator to have some practice in glass blowing. A very effective jet pump can be made from sections of gas pipe. The large chamber should be about 4 or 5 in. long. This should be capped, and holes should be drilled through the ends for inserting the smaller tubes. The open end of the upper tube A, Figure 33, should extend somewhat below the side opening. By attaching a rubber tube to the side tube, a jet pump of this sort can be used as an exhaust pump in connection with laboratory experiments. It can also be used as a suction pump to remove liquids from containing vessels, as from a laundry tub. 

2. Study the operation of a small cistern lift pump. 

If possible, get a discarded pump and remove the valves. If these are out of order, they can be repaired by using circular strips of leather. See if you can put the pump in working order. 

3. Read the gas meter in your home. Be sure to compare your results with those of the gas company. 

4. Make a simple barometer. A simple barometer that indicates but does not measure change in pressure can be made from a flask, a rubber stopper, and a short piece of glass tubing. Draw one end of the tube into a jet, and fit the tube in the hole of the rubber stopper. Fill the flask partly full of water and insert the stopper with the large end of the tube in the water. Support the flask by means of a ringstand and clamp. If the pressure of the air in the flask above the water is greater than the outside pressure, water will drip from the jet. If the pressure inside is less than that outside, air bubbles will rise through the water. Check the changes in your "barometer" with the readings on a standard barometer. 

5. Keep a record of the weather. Keep a record of the barometric and temperature readings over a period of a few weeks, say a month. Observe the weather changes and check to see whether you can find any relation between your readings and the changes in the weather. Keep a record of the weather forecasts in the daily paper, and check these forecasts against the weather.

If daily weather maps are available, check the progress of a high or low from day to day. 

6. Examine a milking machine. Visit a large dairy that uses a milking machine. Learn how the machine operates. 

7. The use of compressed gas in oil wells, Oil flows from many wells due to pressure of gas above the oil. See whether you can find from a geology text the structure of the earth at the place where the oil is found, and how the gas can be effective in lifting the oil.

24. Why Were Your Gymnasium Bleachers Made Strong Enough to Support More People Than Will Ever Sit on Them? 

When your basketball team makes a score, which ties a hard-fought game, you and your friends no doubt suddenly rise from a sitting position to a standing position. This sudden rising helps you to express your feelings of excitement; it also exerts an added force to the floor and supports of your gymnasium. The force exerted by your weight when you are sitting still (dead weight) may be increased by as much as 60 per cent when you suddenly rise (live weight). This means that a person whose dead weight is 125 pounds may by suddenly rising exert a live weight of 200 pounds. You can readily see what a tremendous force may be exerted by a crowd of people suddenly rising during the exciting part of a ball game. 

Why don't the bleachers crash when you have an exciting ball game? Why doesn't a tall building fall when a wind storm strikes it?

Topics to read about. Dead and live weight loads-Tenacity and tensile strength-Factor of safety-Elasticity-Hooke's law. 

How do engineers compute the "load" for gymnasium bleachers or for skyscrapers? What do engineers mean by the term "load"? 

1. A load on a structure is the which the structure must withstand. 

2. When the load is at rest, it is called a weight load. 

3. When the load is in motion, as a person suddenly standing when the home team scores, the load is called a weight load.

The weight of the bleachers themselves is also a load which cannot be neglected. 

4. Where must a skyscraper or a bridge be made the strongest, at the top or the bottom? 

Why must engineers consider the "factor of safety"? When a designer has computed the weight of a structure and the weight of the load it will have to carry, which together may be called the computed load, he then estimates the safety load; that is, he selects material which is several times as strong as that required to carry the computed load. 

5. In addition to these two loads, what are some of the other forces that should be taken into ac- count? 

6. Factor of safety = safety load/ load.

In the case of a certain structure the computed load was 20 tons; the structure was made strong enough to carry a safety load of 100 tons. 

7. The factor of safety in this case is 

8. If the factor of safety provided for in this structure had been 4, what load in tons would be safe for it to carry?

14. Why do engineers need to know the tensile strengths of materials? 

Why do engineers consider the elastic limits of materials when they plan safe gymnasium bleachers? You can squeeze a toy balloon so that it becomes smaller, but, as soon as you stop squeezing, the toy balloon springs back to its original volume. In describing this, the scientist says that the gas in the balloon is perfectly elastic. All gases are perfectly elastic. A solid object is also perfectly elastic within what is called its elastic limit. 

If an automobile spring that gives you a "soft car ride" or a door spring that closes a screen door, is stretched beyond the elastic limit, it will not return to its original condition when the distorting force is removed. -65-

Furthermore, if the boards in your gymnasium bleachers are stretched beyond their elastic limits, they will not return to their original condition after a crowd stands suddenly. 

When a board from your bleachers or when any object is stretched, bent, twisted, or compressed, it is said to be distorted. 

15. The force, which causes the is called the distorting force. 

16. The elasticity of the boards in your bleachers tends to make them recover their shape or original condition after being 17. If an object tends to return to its original condition after being distorted, is it distorted within its elastic limit or beyond its elastic limit? 

18. If a board does not recover its original condition, it is said to be distorted its elastic limit.

In building structures of any kind, engineers attempt to secure the greatest possible strength and yet use as small quantities of materials as possible.

According to the molecular theory: 

I. All matter is made up of molecules. 

II. These molecules are in rapid motion. 

III. Molecules have an attraction for one another. 

IV. Heating any material increases the speeds of the molecules and causes them to spread farther apart.

The following phenomena can be explained by the molecular theory: (a) capillary action, (b) cohesion, (c) adhesion, (d) surface tension, (e) diffusion, (f) expansion, (g) elasticity.

27. If your car stops suddenly, do you fall forward or backward; why?

A car that is stopped tends to stay stopped, and a car that is running tends to keep on running in the same direction.

Force must be applied to a car if its speed or direction is changed.

The ground pushes up against a moving automobile as much as the automobile pushes down against the ground.

The damage to a car in a collision depends largely on its speed and its mass.

Nearly everyone knows that a falling baseball or stone does not move downward with a uniform speed but that it falls faster and faster as it moves downward. On the other hand, if a baseball is thrown upward, its speed decreases continually until the ball reaches its highest point. 

A baseball at rest has no speed. If the ball is allowed to fall for one sec., its speed at the end of the sec. is 32 ft. per sec. During the next second, the speed of the ball again increases 32 ft. per sec.

It does not take long for automobiles to kill as many of our people as have been killed in all the wars of United States history. For some reason people do not consider seriously the dangers of the automobile. Court records show that high school students have their full share of traffic accidents. 

Many automobile accidents could be avoided if drivers understood and appreciated properly the laws of motion. These laws of motion were first stated by 

Sir Isaac Newton in 1687.

As an airplane flies through the air, the engine drives the plane forward, and the resistance of the air pushes against the plane in the opposite direction. 

A head wind may retard the plane, a tail wind may help the engine in pushing it forward, or a cross wind may push it sidewise.

What is resolution? Resolution is the process of finding the components of a quantity that can be represented graphically. 

Composition is the process of finding the resultant when two or more forces, velocities, etc. are given. 

Resolution is just the reverse of this; it is the process of finding the components when the force or velocity is given.

32. How Can You Use the Force of Gravity to Keep Time?

Almost every day we hear these common scientific expressions: frequency, cycle, amplitude, period. 

What do they mean? You can understand the meaning of these terms through a study of the pendulum. 

A pendulum has vibratory motion which is the kind of motion we find related to sound, light, and electricity. If you ask the man who reads your electric meter what kind of current you are using, he will probably tell you that it is "60-cycle" current, which means that it alternates (vibrates) back and forth, moving in each direction 60 times a second.

When you tune in your radio, you simply match the vibration frequency in your radio with that of the broadcasting station. 

What does your radio announcer mean when he says, "This station has a frequency of 770 kilocycles per second"? How can you use a swinging weight to keep time for you?

Topics to read about. Simple pendulum- 

Compound pendulum-Amplitude of vibration, period, and frequency-Laws of the pendulum-Center of oscillation and center of percussion-Mechanics of the clock pendulum. 

How can a simple pendulum keep time for you? If you sit in a swing and someone gives you a push, the swing will be set in motion, vibrating back and forth. The swing is a pendulum; you are the "bob." If you sit perfectly still, the swings or vibrations "die down." If you count the number of vibrations per minute, you will make the interesting discovery that no matter whether the swing vibrates through an arc of a few feet or only a few inches, the time required for each swing or vibration is nearly the same.

Although this simple fact of physics has always been true-ever since the world began-it was not noticed until it was discovered by the great Italian scientist, Galileo (1564-1642). While he was in the 

Cathedral of Pisa, Galileo noticed a swinging chandelier and timed its vibrations with his pulse. 

A small metal sphere or a lead bullet suspended by means of a fine thread is a close approximation to a simple pendulum (Figure 62).

Suspend the weight B from some fixed point O. 

The weight is called the bob. Start the bob swinging. 

Count the number of times the bob swings past the center in one direction in one minute. 

1. The number of times the bob passes the center in one direction in one minute Again set the pendulum swinging and count the number of times the bob passes the center in one direction in one minute.

Figure 62

3. The average number of swings in one direction. in one second 

4. The distance the pendulum swings from the center or lowest point to the outside is called the

How does the force of gravity affect the period of a pendulum? The to-and-fro motion of a pendulum is caused by the force of gravity.

10. In which place will the period of a pendulum of a given length be the longer, in New York City near the seashore or in Denver, near the Rocky Mountains? 

11. It can be shown experimentally as well as mathematically that the period varies inversely as square root of the acceleration due to. the to......... 

Why should you never hit a baseball at the very end of the bat? When you are batting a ball you no doubt have learned by experience that there is a point near the end of the bat where the most effective blow can be delivered. You are conscious of -87-

this because there is practically no jarring effect on the hands. This point is called the center of percussion. The center of percussion is the point in a swinging object at which the most effective blow can be struck. If the ball strikes the bat at a point very near the end of the bat, that is, beyond the center of percussion, a distinct jar is felt in the palm of one hand; and if the ball strikes the bat at the point between the center of percussion and the hands, a jar is experienced in the palm of the other hand. The head of a hammer or a sledge is made heavy in order to get the center of percussion near the striking end.

If you will think of a baseball bat as being a pendulum, you will be able to see why a good ball player is particular about just where on the bat he hits the ball. 

A baseball bat used as a pendulum would be a compound pendulum. In order to find the period of a compound pendulum, it is necessary to find the length of an equivalent simple pendulum; and to do this, it is necessary to find the point in the bat which corresponds to the bob of a simple pendulum, which will vibrate at the same rate as the bat. 

This point is called the center of oscillation. 

Suspend a meter stick by means of a stiff wire through a hole at the end, as shown in Figure 63. 

Now, by the side of the stick suspend a small metal weight by means of a fine thread. 

Board free to swing at 0. Pendulum vibrating at same rate as board.

Figure 63 

Length of pendulum adjusted to vibrate at same rate as bat.

Figure 64

Pass the thread once or twice around the wire so that the length of the string can be lengthened or shortened at will. Set both the simple pendulum and meter stick vibrating and change the length of the string until both pendulums are in unison. 

Note the point on the meter stick which is nearest the center of the bob of the simple pendulum.

33. How Can You Control an Old-Fashioned Clock? 

CONTROLLED LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To appreciate the meaning of a controlled experiment; to find what effect the size of the bob, the length of the swing or arc, and the length of the pendulum have on its rate of vibration. 

Apparatus. Metal balls of different masses and diameters-Fine thread-Ring stand-Clamp with square jaws-Watch, stop watch, or clock that indicates seconds. 

How to get ready for this activity. An old-fashioned pendulum clock which keeps good time in winter may run too slowly in summer. Few such clocks keep correct time consistently. How can one which is not keeping accurate time be adjusted so that it will keep accurate time?

It is suggested that the class be divided into three groups with each group performing one part of this controlled experiment. The factors which may affect the rate of vibration of a pendulum are: (a) the size of the bob; (b) the amplitude; (c) the length. 

Group one will control factors (b) and (c), and vary (a). Group two will control factors (a) and (c), and vary (b). Group three will control factors (a) and (b), and vary (c).

In an experiment like this when one group is responsible for testing the results produced by a certain factor, it becomes necessary for all groups to agree on certain controls. For example, if all groups agree to use a certain length for the pendulums, it will be easier to draw conclusions. A length of 100 cm is satisfactory. The only group that varies the length is the one experimenting with factor (c), All groups should use the same amplitude, except the one group which will vary the amplitude. All groups should use small bobs or metal balls. One group will vary the size of the bobs but will use the other controls set up by the group. 

Amplitude ↑ 

Length of pendulum

Variable factors <Size of bob 

Center of gravity

Figure 65 What to do. Hang a small, metal ball by means of a fine thread to a fixed support so that the ball (bob) is free to swing. Measure accurately the length of the pendulum i.e., the distance from the point of support to the center of gravity of the bob. Start the pendulum swinging through a small arc and determine the time for the pendulum to complete 100 vibrations, and hence the time for one vibration, or the period. 

Repeat using 90 vibrations. Repeat using 110 vibrations. Smaller numbers of vibrations can be used, but the larger the numbers the smaller the error in the period. Take the average of the three periods as the period of the pendulum.

Individual and Group Projects is 

1. Hydraulic ram. A hydraulic ram, or pump, sometimes used to lift water to a height above a given source. The hydraulic ram is operated by a stream of water. If such a device is in use in your neighborhood, read what your textbook says about it, and then study the machine in action. How many valves has a hydraulic ram? What causes them to open and close? What law of motion is exemplified in the operation of this machine? What is the object of the domelike air chamber? 

2. Babcock milk tester. If a Babcock milk tester is available in the laboratory or in your neighborhood, make a study of it, and explain in detail how this centrifugal machine separates milk from cream. Find out how the scale on the test tubes is used. 

3. Compensating pendulum. In some jewelry establishments, clocks having compensating pendulums are used to keep accurate time. These pendulums are 

Speed, Velocity, and Acceleration of various kinds. Some consist of a combination of metal rods so arranged as to expand at different rates with changes of temperature; others have a bob made of a heavy glass vessel, which is partly filled with mercury. A compensating pendulum must be so made that the distance from the point of suspension to the center of gravity of the bob is constant. Find out how this is accomplished.

34. How Much Work Can You Do? 

Shoveling snow, hoeing a garden, running with a football, building a house-all such activities can rightfully be called work. If you work, you move something. Whenever work is done, some object is moved some distance by some force. Try as you may, you do not work if you do not move something. 

What is work? How can the amount of work that a man or a machine does be measured? How do the various units of work differ from one another? 

Topics to read about. Work-Measurement of work-Gravitational and absolute units of work. 

It takes more than force to do work—the force must move something. Whenever a force is exerted to move an object through some distance, work is done. The measure of work done is-

Gravitational units. 

16. In the English system the gravitational unit of work commonly used is the foot pound (ft. lb.), which is the equivalent of the work done by a force of lb. moving an object through a distance of ft. in the direction of the applied force. 

17. In the metric system the gravitational unit of work commonly used is the gram centimeter (gm cm), which is the equivalent of the work done by a force of gm moving an object in the direction of the applied force. cm A force of 10 lb. moves an object 20 ft. in the direction of the force. 35. How Can You Measure How Fast You Work? 

A bent bow has the possibility of shooting an arrow, a stretched spring can lift a weight, and gasoline in the tank of an airplane can "drive" it through the air. In each case the object has the ability to do work and is said to possess energy. Light, heat, and electrical energy are common forms of energy. Heat is used to operate engines, and electrical energy is used to turn motors. The engines and motors do work for us. The energy used in operating them is derived from natural sources, such as fuel or falling water.

36. Why Should a Machine Be Balanced Properly to Do Work?

One of the problems involved in the construction of a machine is that of equilibrium, i.e., the balancing of one part of the machine against another. We are all familiar with the fact that a small child on one end of a seesaw can balance a full-grown man on the other end, provided the fulcrum, or support, is placed sufficiently near the man. This is precisely what an engineer does when he designs a machine. A small force at some distance from the point of support can be made to balance a large force near the support. 

When such a system is in balance, it is said to be in equilibrium. 

What makes the engine of a car vibrate? Why do you brace yourself when somebody tries to push you?

Topics to read about. Center of gravity-Moment of a force-Equilibrium-Three states of equilibrium-Conditions for stability. 

A machine part can be balanced so that it will rotate without vibrating. You no doubt have heard people say that a wheel is not balanced properly. What does this mean?

3.

This means that your finger is directly under the of the stick. 

4. The center of gravity of an object is the point at which it can be.... 

5. The center of gravity of a rod of uniform crosssectional area and density is at the 6. The center of gravity of a ball bat is nearer the end.

The stability of a machine can be increased by lowering its "center of gravity." The most recent automobiles were built with their "centers of gravity" near the ground. The center of gravity might be called the "center of weight". When the center of gravity of a machine, or of a building, or of anything, is high, its stability may be low. Frequently the base of a skyscraper is made much broader than the top of the building. Examples are: the Empire State Building of New York City; the 

Tribune Tower of Chicago; the Carew Tower of Cincinnati; the Terminal Tower of Cleveland.

37. How Long a Pole Do You Need to Lift One End of a Five-Ton Truck? 

You use handle bars to steer your bicycle. The handle bars form a simple machine called a lever. 

Any rigid bar can be used as a simple machine to do work. 

 How can you overcome a large resistance with a small force? How long a pole do you need to lift one end of a 5-ton truck?

Topics to be read about. What a machine is and what it does-The principle of work-Mechanical advantage-Efficiency-Levers and their parts. 

How can you measure the work that can be done with a pole or with any simple machine?

If you were to use a pole to lift one end of a 5-ton truck, you would use the pole to increase force. You might put the pole over a log so that when you push down, the end of the 5-ton truck would go up, that is, you would use the pole to change the direction of force. In addition, you would be pushing on a pole at one end, but the other end of the pole would be "doing the lifting" on the truck, that is, the point of application of the force would be changed. A machine can be used easily but is more difficult to define. But let's try: A machine is a device for transferring energy or transforming energy; or, a machine is a device for changing the direction of a force, the size of a force, or the point of application of a force. The steam engine is an illustration of the transforming of the energy in fuels to the energy of motion.

The ideal or simple machine is a frictionless machine. Of course, no such machine exists. We talk about it in order to illustrate the fundamental principles which you should know about machines.

39. Why Do Our Modern Machines Use Levers? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To find how two or more forces can be balanced on a lever. 

Apparatus. Meter stick-Sliding knife edge 

Fulcrum support-Set of weights-Beam balance- String.

How to get ready for this activity. All our modern machines are made up of the different simple machines including levers. Why do we use levers? You may answer, "We use levers to do more work than we could do otherwise." But an answer of this kind does not tell us how long the different levers in your automobile should be or how strong they should be. 

The intricate machine of today, whether it is a motor car or a sewing machine, must be well planned by skilled people. 

A lever enables you to balance one set of forces with another set of forces. The tendency of any force to produce rotation is called the moment of force. 

The moment of a force is the product of the force and its lever arm. In Figure 78 the lever arm of the force E is which is the distance from the fulcrum to the force, measured at right angles to the direction of the force. 1, 12 Repeat, using weights of 100 gm and 200 gm.

Figure 78

An ideal, or simple lever, is a rigid, weightless bar. 

In actual practice, however, any lever has weight, which must be taken into consideration. The center of gravity is a point at which we can consider all the mass of the object to be concentrated. Therefore, you can consider the weight of the meter stick as applied at this point. If the fulcrum is at the center of gravity, the support pushes up with a force equal to the pull of gravity in the downward direction; hence, the weight of the lever need not be considered. 

What to do. Balance the meter stick at the center of gravity. Hang a 200-gm weight at a distance of 30 cm on one side of the fulcrum, and balance this with 200 gm on the opposite side of the fulcrum. 40. How Many Pulley Wheels Do You Need to Lift Your Grand Piano to the Second Floor? 

It would be impossible to lift a piano to the second floor by using a lever. Long levers are heavy and cumbersome. They cannot be used easily to lift objects to any great distance.

Since it is not practical to attempt to move resistances long distances with bars as levers, man learned to use the pulley and combinations of pulleys to act as "continuous levers." 

 How can pulleys be arranged to lift heavy objects several feet? How many pulley wheels do you need to lift your grand piano to the second floor?

Topics to read about. Fixed pulleys-Movable pulleys-Combinations of pulleys.

How many pulleys do you need? In lifting a load with a combination of pulleys you may pull down or up depending upon whether one end of the string, or rope, is fastened to the fixed pulley block, or to the movable pulley block. The mechanical advantage of a system of pulleys is determined by the number of strands of rope that overcome the resistance. If the resistance overcome is a load being lifted, the weight of the movable pulley block must be added to the weight in order to obtain the resistance.

41. Why Does a "Steam Shovel" Use Pulleys?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To find the mechanical advantage and efficiency of each of a number of pulley systems. 

Apparatus. Pulleys suitable for three or four systems-Meter stick-Beam balance-Set of weights -Ring stand-Clamps-String. 

How to get ready for this activity. A pulley system is one of the most convenient and effective machines for multiplying force. It may be used by the man who needs to lift a grand piano or by the telephone lineman who stretches wires tightly from pole to pole or by the man who operates a "steam shovel." 

The resistance that is overcome by a pulley system depends upon the number of pulleys used, and upon how the pulleys are combined.

A fixed pulley may be fastened to a beam or a pole or a ceiling. A fixed pulley can "turn around" or rotate, but it cannot travel "up or down." The scientist describes the fixed pulley by saying it is free to rotate but not free to move in a linear direction. A pulley that has both rotary and linear motion is a movable pulley.

What to do. 

WILL A SINGLE FIXED PULLEY HELP You? 

Hang a weight of at least 1 kg on one end of the cord of the single fixed pulley of Figure 85A. Find the effort necessary to lift R with uniform speed. 

This can be done by using a spring balance, or by hanging the weight holder on the end of the string and adding weights. The more accurate method is to hang known weights to the end of the string. Call this force, "the effort up," Eu. This Eu must overcome friction as well as lift R. Now find the effort necessary to let R down with uniform speed. Call this the "effort down," Ea. Friction now helps E in letting R down. The average of E, and Ea is the value of E if there is no friction.

43. How Can You Lift a House With Your Hands by Using an Inclined Plane?

With a good axe a heavy board or a log can be split. By means of a long plank heavy barrels of paint or chemicals or other materials can be loaded into a truck. By avoiding steep grades railroads are able to transport goods to all parts of our country. 

In each of these cases some form of inclined plane is used.

Apparatus. Inclined plane-Small can for holding shot-Car-Small shot or sand-Meter stick Ring stand with clamp and rod-Beam balance-Set of weights. 

How to get ready for this activity. The lever probably was the first machine ever invented. It was followed by the inclined plane. Anyone who looks at the great blocks of stone in the pyramids of Egypt wonders how such heavy stones were lifted into place. 

The task would be easy today with our modern hoisting machines. But the early Egyptians had no such powerful and complicated devices. They probably used inclined planes. 30° 

Figure 94

What to do. Arrange the plane as shown in Figure 94, the plane making an angle of about 30° with the horizontal. Place a clamp on the table at A to 

prevent the plane from sliding during the experiment. 

Add a load of 1 kg or 2 kg to the car to increase R. 

Place the car near the bottom of the plane and add shot, or sand, to the can until the car moves up the plane with uniform speed. Help the car start by tapping the plane lightly. Weigh the can and its contents. Call this the effort up, Eu. Remove shot from the can until, with light tapping, the car descends at uniform speed. Weigh the can and contents and call this the effort down, Ed. The average of Eu and Ea is the effort necessary to move the load up the plane without friction. Weigh the car and its contents and call this the resistance R. Measure the length / and height h of the plane. The length should be measured on the underside of the plane from the point A where the plane touches the table. Since / and h are to be compared, it is not necessary to measure the entire length of the plane. Any distance along the plane can be taken as /, if h is measured at the same point. A convenient length for / is 1 meter.

When the effort E moves the car a distance / along the plane, the work accomplished has been that of lifting the car and contents R a height h against gravity. If there is no friction, no work is done in moving R the horizontal distance AB. Determine the mechanical advantage of the plane, and the ratio of l/h. 

Consider that the useful work done is that of lifting the load in the car through the height h. The actual work done to accomplish this is that done by E, acting through a distance 7. Compute the efficiency of the plane at this angle. 

Change the angle of inclination and repeat the measurements. Trial 1. Trial 1 2. Trial 2
