45. In What Ways Does Friction Help Our Modern Machines; 

In What Ways Does Friction Hinder Them? "Don't back your car onto that ice or you'll have to push!" Without friction it would be impossible to start your car or stop it. Friction, however, tends to wear out moving parts of a car. The tires wear out; the bearings do the same. Your car may cause trouble because the pistons, the crankshaft bearings, or some other movable parts do not fit properly. 

What parts of a car need as much friction as possible? 

What parts of a car need as little friction as possible? 

In what ways is friction decreased? In what way does it add to the cost of doing work?

How Well Do You Think? 

The following statements apply to a machine. (a) In a frictionless machine the work done by the machine is equal to the work done on the machine. Output = input. (b) The efficiency of a machine is equal to the output divided by the input. Because of friction, efficiency is usually less than 100 per cent. (c) A machine may be used in such a way that the resistance and the effort used to overcome the resistance, move in opposite directions. (d) A machine may be used to overcome a large resistance with a small effort. (e) A machine may be used to make the resistance move at a greater speed than the effort. (f) The work done by a machine cannot be greater than the work done on the machine.

46. If All the Thermometers in the World Were Smashed,

How Could You Measure Temperature? 

The normal temperature of your body is 98.6 degrees. Let it drop to about 96 degrees or climb to 

109 degrees, and death promptly ensues. A thermometer aids the doctor in restoring you to health.

It serves the scientist and the engineer. A thermometer is an important instrument. 

What is "hotness" or "coldness"? How does temperature differ from heat? If all the thermometers in the world were smashed, how could you make a new one?

8. The principle upon which a thermometer is based is that a body changes if its temperature changes. 

You can make a rough thermometer quite easily.

Fit a glass tube in a one-hole rubber stopper, and place the stopper in the mouth of a flask (Figure 97).

Fill the flask and tube with water to the height shown.

Place the flask successively in lukewarm water and in hot water, and note the height of the water in the tube in each case. Temperature and heat are related to molecular motion or molecular energy. 

1. The temperature of an object indicates the averof its molecules. age 2. The heat in an object is the total of its molecules. 

3. If a cup of water and a barrel of water have the same temperature, which has the greater amount of heat? 

Can you measure temperature by a sense of touch? Long before the thermometer was invented, people tried to guess at the hotness or coldness of the weather, or of fever patients, by the sense of touch. Such a method is quite unreliable. Try this experiment: Fill one basin with water, which feels quite hot; fill a second basin with tepid water; fill a third basin with ice water. Put your hand into the hot water for a few moments and then into the tepid water. 

4. Does the tepid water feel warm or cool?

Put your hand in the ice water and then into the same tepid water. 

5. Now does your hand feel warm or cool? 

6. Judging from this experiment, is the temperature sense a good means or a poor means of determining temperature?

47. A Pound of Iron Heats Up Quicker Than a Pound of Water Over the Same Flame. Why? 

Back in the days of your great-great-grandmother you would not have had any fine furnace to keep you warm. If your feet were cold at night, you could choose between a bottle of hot water or a hot "flat iron." Your great-great-grandmother soon learned to choose the hot water rather than the hot iron. You can understand why if you know that whether you are talking about hot water or hot iron, or any other material, the amount of heat given up by any object in cooling depends not only upon its temperature change and its mass, but also upon how much heat is given up by one unit of mass when the temperature falls one degree.

How can you measure specific heat? According to the law of conservation of energy, heat cannot be created or destroyed, but can be changed to some other form. This principle indicates that whatever heat is lost by one object is gained by something else. -131-

If the material of a warm object is mixed with the material of a cool object, the cool material will absorb as much heat as the warm material sets free. 

This is known as the law of mixtures. An application of this method enables one to determine the specific heat of a material, or the final temperature resulting when hot and cold objects are mixed. 

If 1,000 gm of mercury at 20°C are mixed with 33 gm of water at 40°C, the resulting temperature is 

30°C. Find the specific heat of the mercury. Let x = specific heat of mercury.

48. Why Does a Doctor Prescribe the Use of a Hot-Water Bottle in Preference to a Piece of Hot Metal? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose.

To determine the specific heat of a metal. 

Apparatus. Metal pellets of lead, copper, and aluminum-Beam balance-Two thermometers- 

Calorimeter, with a metal cup to fit in it-Boiler— 

Bunsen burner. 

How to get ready for this activity. Only a short time is required to heat a heavy, iron skillet. A much longer time is required to heat an equal mass of water. No two materials require the same quantity of heat to raise the temperature of a given mass the same number of degrees. This is the same as saying that no two materials have the same specific heat. 

The specific heat of water is 1; that is, one gm of water requires 1 calorie per centigrade degree that its temperature is raised.

A convenient way to find the specific heat of a material is to use the "method of mixtures". This method utilizes the principle of the conservation of energy. Hot material is mixed with cold material in an insulated container. If all the heat is retained within the container, the heat given out by the hot material is absorbed by the cold material. 

A given mass of any one kind of material gives out just as much heat when the temperature falls a certain number of degrees as is required to raise the temperature the same amount. 

What to do. Fill the boiler of Figure 99 about two-thirds full of water and start heating it. While the water is heating, weigh out 500 gm of lead shot 

and pour this into cup C. Place C in the opening of the boiler. Place a thermometer with its bulb near the center of the shot and allow the metal to heat until the temperature becomes constant. In the meantime, weigh the inner cup of the calorimeter. (The mass of the outer cup is not needed, since this cup merely keeps in the jacket of insulating air.)

Figúre 99 

Weigh out about 200 gm of water at a temperature several degrees below that of the room, say 15°C to 17°C, and pour into the calorimeter. When the temperature of the metal has become constant for a few minutes, read this temperature to the nearest tenth of a degree. Stir the water in the calorimeter and take its temperature with the second thermometer as accurately as that of the metal. Remove the cup with the metal from the boiler and quickly pour the shot into the water, being careful not to splash out any of the water. Stir the metal-water mixture gently until an equilibrium temperature is reached. 

Read this temperature to the nearest tenth of a degree.

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the coefficient of linear expansion of a solid.

Apparatus. Steam generator-Bunsen burner— 

Thermometer-Micrometer caliper-Tubes of brass, copper, aluminum, and glass-Meter stick-Two wooden blocks, one with thin metal strip fastened to one edge.

When the fire grates in your furnace become heated, they expand. For this reason, the manufacturer makes them so that they fit rather loosely in the furnace. In a similar manner does the glass in your windows get longer on warm days and shorter on cold days? If so, how much? How can you determine the coefficient of expansion of a metal, or glass, or any other solid material? 

How to get ready for this activity. The coefficient of linear expansion of a solid tells you the change in length that each unit length undergoes for each degree change in temperature. The original length must be measured at 0°C for scientific accuracy. However, you can measure the original length in your work at room temperature; there should be no significant error as a result of this. As you perform this experiment, measure the expansion carefully since the amount of expansion is very small in comparison with the original length of the solid. 

Here is a convenient method for keeping a metal bar at a specific temperature long enough to measure its expansion: use a tube made of the metal and send steam through the tube. If you allow one end of this tube to move over a rod attached to a pointer, a small expansion can be magnified for easier reading. 

What to do. Draw one end of a piece of glass tubing into a long capillary tube, making the capillary tube at least 30 cm long. Bend this part of the tube at right angles to the remainder of the tube. 

Measure the length of the capillary tube. With the micrometer caliper, measure the diameter of the undrawn portion of the tube. Place the tube on a block of smooth wood with a meter scale behind the tip of the capillary tube as shown in Figure 102. Place a metal tube, whose coefficient is to be determined, in position with one end on the metal strip and the other end on the glass tube. 

Attach the metal tube to the hose on the steam generator. This end of the metal tube is held in position by a rubber band, or better still, by a narrow groove filed in it. The other end of the metal is free to move, but when it does expand, it moves the glass pointer. Measure the length of the metal tube from 

B to the center of A. It will simplify your calculations if you make this distance 100 cm. 

In the open end of the metal tube place the bulb of a thermometer, and read the temperature. Adjust the capillary tube to an almost vertical position with the end on some scale division on the meter scale. 

Read this position. Boil the water in the generator, and send steam through the tube for a few minutes.

Read the new position of the capillary tube on the scale to tenths of a millimeter. Read the final temperature.

51. How Cold Is an Object That Can Never Be Made Any Colder? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the relation between the volume and the absolute temperature of a gas, if the pressure remains constant.

Apparatus. Steam boiler - Bunsen burner 

Large beaker-Charles' law tube-Thermometer—

Ice-Salt. 

How to get ready for this activity. Ordinary temperatures are measured by the expansion and contraction of a liquid, such as mercury or alcohol. 

A thermometer of this kind is limited, however, to the temperature range between the freezing and boiling points of the liquid used. Ordinary thermometers have another fault-the liquids in them do not expand and contract at uniform rate.

A gas can be used to overcome these two faults of an ordinary thermometer. A gas can be used that does not liquefy until it reaches a very low temperature. Air, for example, liquefies under a pressure of one atmosphere at a temperature of approximately - 190°C. Drop of mercury Air Steam Drop of mercury 

What to do. Remove the tube from the ice and suspend it in steam from boiling water. Determine the volume V2, and the temperature.

Drain the water from the beaker and pack the ice with salt to obtain a temperature several degrees below 0°C. Determine the volume V3 of the air, and its temperature.

Empty the beaker and surround it with some insulating material. Fill the beaker with hot water at some temperature lower than 100°C, say 80°C. Keep the water stirred and find the volume V, and the temperature.

By how much does the volume of a given mass of gas change when the temperature changes by one degree? In this experiment the pressure is kept constant at one atmosphere, and the volume is allowed to change with the temperature.

A glass tube, sealed at one end, has a certain mass of dry air between the closed end of the tube and a drop of mercury. With a change in volume of the enclosed air the mercury drop rises or falls in the tube. If the tube has uniform cross-section, the change in volume is proportional to the change in height of the mercury drop. Thus, the diameter of the tube is not needed.

The temperature of a solid or a liquid is usually measured on the centigrade or Fahrenheit scale. As you do this experiment, use the centigrade thermometer, but when you record the temperature, use the absolute scale. The size of the degree is the same on this scale as it is on the centigrade scale, but zero on the absolute scale is -273°C. Any centigrade reading can be converted to absolute reading by adding 273.

52. Heating a Solid Does Not Always Raise Its Temperature. Why? 

When you feel something cold you frequently say, "It's as cold as ice." This statement is very expressive, but it does not mean much because ice may have various temperatures. Ice can be much colder than 0°C. On a winter day when the temperature of the air is -10°C, for example, the temperature of an icicle is -10°C.

Ice and other crystalline substances, such as salt, sulfur, and copper have definite melting points. Noncrystalline substances, such as sealing wax, paraffin, and butter, have no definite melting points, but gradually soften as they change from the solid to the liquid state.

53. Why Are Cubes of Ice Better for Cooling Summer Beverages Than Are Ice-Cold Chunks of Metal? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the number of calories of heat required to melt one gram of ice. 

Apparatus. Calorimeter-Thermometer-Beam balance-Set of weights-Ice. 

How to get ready for this activity. If heat is applied to any object, the temperature usually rises. 

Heat can be applied with no change in temperature, however, when the melting point of a solid has been reached. If heat is applied to a cake of ice which is at -10°C, the temperature rises until it reaches 0°C, each gram of ice requiring approximately 0.5 calories for each degree change in temperature. When the ice reaches its melting point, however, its temperature stays at 0°C for a time even though heat is still being applied. The 0°C temperature continues until the ice has melted. This fact can be explained by the conclusion that molecules are farther apart in the liquid state than they are in the solid state. Hence, work must be done to "pull" them farther apart against their attractive forces. The energy necessary to pull the molecules in a unit mass of material farther apart is supplied by a specific quantity of heat known as the heat of fusion. 

A convenient method for determining the heat of fusion is the method of mixtures. A cake of ice at 

0°C is placed in some warm water in a calorimeter. 

Heat from the warm water and the calorimeter changes the ice to ice water and raises the temperature of the resulting ice water. The heat lost by the warm water and the calorimeter is equal to the heat gained by the ice and ice water. If the mass, the change in temperature, and the specific heat of water and calorimeter are known, the heat lost can be computed easily. Likewise, the heat gained by the ice water can be computed from its mass and its temperature change. Thus, the heat required to melt all the ice is known. Hence, if the mass of ice is known, the heat required to melt one gram can be found.

What to do. Weigh the calorimeter to determine its mass. Fill the calorimeter about two-thirds full of water at a temperature of 30°C to 35°C and weigh again to determine the mass of the water. Get a piece of ice about the size of a refrigerator ice cube and dry it thoroughly. If the ice has been in the room for several minutes, its temperature can be taken as 0°C. Take the temperature of the water, and place the dry ice in the water immediately, being careful not to spill any water. Stir gently with the thermometer until the ice melts. As soon as the ice melts take the temperature of the mixture. To neutralize heat exchanges between the calorimeter and the air around it, the final temperature should be approximately as much below room temperature as the initial temperature is above room temperature. 

Weigh the calorimeter and water again to determine the mass of ice that melted.

Repeat the experiment using a different mass of water.

55. Why Can a Steam Radiator Give Out More Heat Than a Hot Water Radiator of the Same Capacity? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To measure the number of calories that are given out by one gram of steam in condensing. 

Apparatus. Calorimeter - Steam generator 

Theromometer-Test tube-Two-hole rubber stop per-Glass tubing-Rubber tubing-Ring-stand- 

Beam balance-Set of weights-Bunsen burner. 

How to get ready for this activity. When water in an open pan is boiling, its temperature is 100°C. 

The steam produced also has a temperature of 100°C. 

The temperature of neither the water nor the steam rises beyond 100°C, although heat from the flame is being added to the water continually. This means that the water molecules and the steam molecules are moving at the same speed, since temperature is an indication of molecular movement. Molecules of steam are much farther apart than are molecules of water. Work must be done to pull the molecules of water farther apart to make steam. Thus, all the energy used to vaporize 1 gm of water at 100°C is used in doing this work, and now exists in the form of potential energy of the molecules. 

If steam condenses, the molecules come back to their original distances with respect to one another. 

Hence, their potential energy is decreased, and this energy appears again as heat. The number of calories given out when 1 gm of steam condenses is found to be the same as the number of calories required to vaporize this same mass of water. The number of calories given out by one unit of mass of steam in condensing is known as the heat of condensation of steam.

The heat of condensation of steam can be determined conveniently by the method of mixtures. A known mass of steam is passed into a known mass of water contained in a calorimeter of known mass. In condensing, the steam gives out heat. The resulting water at 100°C also gives out heat in cooling. By the law of conservation of energy all this heat is used to raise the temperature of the cold water and calorimeter, neglecting small losses due to radiation.

STEAM- STEAM full of water. Heat to boiling and allow the steam to pass through the delivery tube for several seconds in order to heat the tubes as nearly as possible to a temperature of 100°C. It is necessary that very little condensed steam leave the opening of the delivery tube. The test tube catches any water that forms between the steam generator and that point. 

Meanwhile determine the mass of the calorimeter. 

Fill the calorimeter about two-thirds full of water at approximately 15°C and determine the mass of the water. Determine the temperature of the steam by holding the thermometer in the steam leaving the delivery tube. Take the temperature of the water in the calorimeter, remove any drops of water from the delivery tube, and immediately place the calorimeter under the delivery tube with the end of the tube beneath the surface of the water. Stir the water continually until the temperature reaches approximately 35°C. Remove calorimeter and stir until an equilibrium temperature is reached. Weigh again to determine the mass of steam condensed in the water. 

Since the final temperature is approximately as much above room temperature as the initial temperature was below room temperature, the calorimeter and contents give approximately the same amount of heat to the room as they receive from the room, thus minimizing heat loss or gain.

2. Mass of calorimeter + water.... 

3. Mass of water (m) 

4. Mass of calorimeter + water+steam.. 

5. Mass of steam (m.) 

6. Initial temperature of water (tw)...... 

7. Initial temperature of calorimeter (t.). 

8. Temperature of steam (t.). 

9. Temperature of mixture (tm) 

10. Specific heat of calorimeter

Note. The heat of condensation of steam can be computed from the following relation:

Heat absorbed by cold water+heat absorbed by calorimeter = heat set free by steam in condensing (no change in temperature) +heat set free by hot water in cooling.

59. How Does the Engine in Your Car Work? 

You know that when a gun is fired, an explosion occurs and the bullet is expelled. Powder is burned in the gun producing a gas at a very high temperature. The violent expansion of this gas drives the bullet from the gun.

In a similar way fuel is burned in each cylinder of a gas engine, and the hot, expanding gas pushes the piston. Since the fuel is burned in the cylinder, this engine would get very hot if we did not use some special cooling method. In your automobile this is done by circulating water around the cylinders. 

 Why does the liquid in the radiator of your car make the motor less efficient? How does a simple gas engine work? What is the difference between a four-cycle engine and a two-cycle engine?

The greater the temperature, the greater the pressure of a gas enclosed in a can or in the cylinder of a "gasoline motor". The scientist expresses this fact precisely by saying that the pressure of a gas is proportional to its absolute temperature. You must add 

273 to the centigrade reading if you wish to know the absolute temperature. For example, a gas at 

0°C has a temperature of 273° on the absolute scale.

In the cylinder of your car, if the temperature were to change from 273° to 546° on the absolute scale, the pressure would be doubled. The value can be computed by the following equation in which we assume that the initial temperature is 15 lb. per in.2. 15 273 546; ; hence, x=30 lb. per in.2.

60. How Can You Proceed to Solve a Problem Scientifically? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To learn to carry on a controlled laboratory activity. 

Good scientists, almost without exception, are good experimenters. They have learned how to solve problems by means of controlled experiments. They know how to discover truths. Many of their experiments require rather complicated techniques and procedures, but not always. There are many simple problems, which can be solved with simple and inexpensive apparatus.

The purpose of this exercise is to help you learn how to experiment. This means that you must plan your procedures, develop a table for your results, and draw conclusions based on the evidence that you obtain rather than from the reading of books. 

In order to have a controlled experiment, all factors except one must be controlled. One factor only may vary at one time, if you are to be certain that that factor produces the results.

Each part of this exercise is stated as a problem. 

In some cases experimental factors are mentioned.

In others some factors to be controlled are suggested.

1. Make an expansion thermometer. An expansion rod can be made by riveting together a strip of iron and a strip of brass. How does the coefficient of linear expansion of brass compare with that of iron? 

What causes the bar to bend, or “buckle", when heated? Electric contact can be made by means of a thumb screw. When a battery and an electric bell are connected in the circuit, this device can serve as a fire alarm. This expansion apparatus illustrates also the principle of the oven thermostat. 

2. Make a wet-dry bulb hygrometer. Fasten two thermometers to a board as shown in your text or in an encyclopedia. Label one thermometer "dry" and the other "wet". Wrap around the wet bulb a piece of coarse wicking, the lower end of which should dip into a vessel of water. See if you can determine the relative humidity of the room by means of this instrument. 

Heat and Temperature . 

3. Make a fireless cooker. A box with a cover, that can be closed securely, can be used for this purpose. 

The insulating materials supporting the food containers can be asbestos, powdered cork, sawdust, or even hay. Fill one of the containers with hot water. 

Take the temperature of the water. Then close the fireless cooker for several hours. Take the temperature again. This will give some idea of the insulating properties of the cooker.

How Well Do You Think? (1) The quantity of water vapor in the air at any one time is known as absolute humidity. (2) The capacity of air to hold water vapor is increased with an increase in temperature. (3) The quantity of water vapor in the air compared to what it can hold at that temperature is known as relative humidity. (4) The temperature at which the water vapor in the air begins to condense is known as the dew point. (5) The relative humidity is 100 per cent when the air is saturated with water vapor.

61. How Can Magnets Be Used to Show the 

Speed of Your Car?

Early peoples used natural magnets made from an iron ore, called magnetite. Such magnets were called lodestones (leading stones), doubtless because of their ability to "lead" travelers or navigators by pointing in approximately a north-south direction. 

This action was strange to early peoples who knew nothing of magnetism. Magnetic effects are still strange and interesting to modern people. 

What materials are used to make magnets? What are magnetic fields? Are all materials magnetic? 

How do magnets affect one another? Does one end of a compass point exactly north? How do magnets show the speed of your car?

Topics to read about. Properties of magnets— 

Laws of attraction and repulsion-Difference between a magnet and a magnetic material-Magnetic lines of force-Magnetic fields-Magnetic induction-Terrestrial magnetism.

How does one magnet affect another magnet? Suspend a magnet in a wire stirrup attached to a long thread (Figure 112). 

1. In what direction does the magnet set itself?

The end that points toward the north is the northseeking, or N pole; the end that points toward the south is the south-seeking, or S pole.

N pole of the first magnet. 

2. Result: 

Bring the S pole of the second magnet near the N pole of the first magnet.

62. Where Are a Magnet's "Lines of Force"?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To map the magnetic fields between like and unlike poles.

Apparatus. Two bar magnets-U-shaped magnet 

Short bar of iron-Short bar of copper-Two strips of wood as thick as the magnets and somewhat longer than the combined lengths of the bar magnets (sometimes a board is prepared with grooves into which the magnets fit)-Iron filings in a can with small holes in top-Paper or glass plate. 

How to get ready for this activity. Because of the action of compass needles and iron filings in the field about a magnet, we have come to assume that there are lines of force leaving the N pole of a magnet and entering the S pole. These lines of force are not visible but the assumptions that they exist and have direction help us to explain many of the observations concerning magnetism. 

Where are the lines of force of a bar magnet? (Part 1). Lay a bar magnet on the table. On each side and parallel with it place two strips of wood (Figure 117). Lay the glass plate (or sheet of paper) over the magnet, and sprinkle a thin layer of iron filings on the plate. Tap the plate gently with a pencil to help the filings arrange themselves. Sketch on the next page the magnet and the shape of the magnetic field. Label the poles of the magnet and indicate with arrows the direction of the lines of force.

If blueprint paper is available, the filings can be sprinkled on this paper in bright sunlight and left until the uncovered parts of the paper have turned blue. The paper can then be soaked in water a few minutes and dried. This gives an accurate picture of the field. 

If you use a blueprint, paste it on next page. If you do not use a blueprint, make a simple drawing. 

Figure 118 

What happens to the lines of force when like poles are facing each other? Repeat Part 3 by placing the two magnets with the like poles about two inches apart. Put your blueprint or drawing on the next page.

Figure 120

Sprinkle iron filings on a sheet of paper placed over the magnet. What is the shape of the field? Compare with Part 2. Put your blueprints or drawing on the next page.

1. Which offers the better path for lines of force, copper or iron? 

How can the field of a magnet be mapped with a compass? Fasten a sheet of wrapping paper about 2 ft. square to a drawing board or table. Place a bar magnet near the center of the paper and outline its position with a pencil. -169-

Hold a small compass needle near one corner of the N pole of the magnet, but a short distance above the paper. Place a dot on the paper beneath the center of the compass needle. Through this line draw a short, straight line parallel to the compass needle. 

Move the compass a short distance along this line away from the N pole, and draw a dot and line as before. Move the compass along this line and repeat the process. Continue in this manner until the S pole of the magnet is reached. Now join all the dots with a smooth line. This line represents the direction of a line of force. Place an arrow on it to indicate its direction.

63. Why Is It Often Difficult for You to Comb 

Your Hair on a Cold, Dry Day?

When you walk or slide on a soft carpet on a cold, dry day and then hold your finger near a friend, he may hear a tiny sharp, crackling noise, feel a shock, and see a spark jump between the two of you. When you rub a cat's fur, you may see sparks. Instead of staying down, your hair may stand up or spread apart when you comb it on a cold, dry day. Why? 

 What is "static electricity"? What happens to glass when it is rubbed with silk? Can any two substances be rubbed together and become electrified? Why is it often difficult to comb your hair on a cold, dry day?

Purpose. To study the properties and laws of static electricity.

Apparatus. Electroscope-Glass rods-Vulcanite rod-Silk cloth-Woolen cloth-Silk thread-Ring stand with support-Proof plane. 

How to get ready for this activity. When Benjamin Franklin sent his kite up to a cloud that was electrically charged, he performed an exceedingly dangerous experiment. Today it is possible to perform experiments in the laboratory, even with electricity under high tension, with comparative ease and safety.

In order to perform experiments in static electricity successfully the apparatus must be clean, that is, free from dust, and dry. The dry, cold days of winter are better than the moist, warm days of spring for this purpose.

65. What Is the Purpose of the Trailing Chain on a Gasoline Truck?

Before the time of Benjamin Franklin no one had ever shown that a flash of lightning had anything to do with electricity. Franklin proved that lightning is electrical in nature. You can "make a little lightning" of your own by rubbing your feet on a dry carpet and then reaching your finger toward a door knob. What might happen if a gasoline truck "made some lightning?" 

 How are electrical charges "stored up"? What apparatus is used to make sparks jump across long gaps? What is the purpose of the trailing chain on a gasoline truck?

Topics to read about. Electrophorus-Electro static machine-Leyden jar-Potential-Condenser -Capacity of condenser-Distribution of charge on an object-Lightning-Lightning rod. 

How many charges of electricity can be made from one charge? An electrophorus is composed of two parts (Figure 127). Part B is a shallow dish which contains hard rubber, sealing wax, or resin.

Part A is a "proof plane" composed of a metal disc and an insulating handle. Remove the disc and "polish" the rubber (or wax or resin-they all give the same kind of electric charge) with woolen cloth.

How does a condenser store up electric charges? Of course you are familiar with the fact that your radio is "tuned" by turning a knob. This knob turns a set of movable plates so that they go between a set of fixed plates. All the plates, movable and fixed, constitute a condenser. The term condenser sometime leads to misunderstanding of its use. A condenser is a device for increasing or decreasing capacity to hold a charge at a given potential. 

Make a condenser by fastening two metal conductors (sheets of tin, zinc, or copper) a few inches square, to wooden supports as shown in Figure 128. 

Use a wire to connect plate C to the electroscope A. 

Also, connect plate D to the ground G through a water pipe or other earth connection. The electroscope A indicates the potential of the condenser. 

The object of connecting D to the ground is to provide a path for electrons to move on to D or away from D. Set the plates a short distance apart. 

With a proof plane, charge plate C.

12. Does this indicate a rise or fall of potential?

Individual and Group Projects 

1. Measure the angle of magnetic dip in your locality.

To perform this exercise it is necessary to have a laboratory dipping needle, which is delicately balanced at its middle point. Set the dipping needle at various places about the school yard. Be sure that there are no magnetic objects, such as pocketknives, near it at the time the reading is taken. 

2. Measure the angle of declination. On an evening when the sky is clear, by means of the Big Dipper find the position of the North Star, which determines very closely the direction of the north geographic pole. Set two stakes in the ground in such positions that a string stretched from one to the other will 

Magnetism point to the north star. The string lies on a geographic meridian, that is to say, it points in a true north-south direction. Now place a long magnetic needle just below the string. The angle made by the needle and the string is the angle of declination for your locality. What is the line of no declination? Are you east or west of this line?

67. What Determines the Kind of Service a Battery 

Will Give You?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To study the factors that determine the voltage and current that a cell or battery can deliver.

Apparatus. Glass tumbler-Plate holder-Di lute sulfuric acid-Voltmeter-Ammeter - Small quantity of mercury-Copper wire-Strips of carbon, copper, zinc, lead-Solution of table salt (sodium chloride)-strong solution of chromic acid. 

How to get ready for this activity. Most of the electric current used in your home and community is produced by generators, but in order to supply current to such devices as a flashlight, voltaic cells are used. In such a cell the voltage is produced by chemical action. The current supplied by a cell depends upon the voltage of the cell. 

A common type of voltaic cell is the familiar dry cell, which really is not dry, but contains a solution. 

The amount of current that a dry cell can deliver decreases as the cell ages. Even in a new cell the current decreases rapidly if the cell is delivering a large current. Therefore, dry cells cannot be used if a large, steady current is needed. 

If one cell cannot provide adequate voltage or current, cells of any kind can be connected in a group to form a battery. Your flashlight battery may have two, three, or more cells; the battery in your automobile probably contains three cells. 

What is local action in a cell; how is it prevented? Fill a glass tumbler about two-thirds full of dilute sulfuric acid (1 volume of acid to about 10 volumes of water). Place a copper (Cu) strip in the solution and note the effect. Record your observation after number 1 in "What I observed".

Place the zinc (Zn) strip in the solution and note the effect. Record your observations after number 2 in "What I observed". If gas is formed freely at the plate, it is due to impurities in the Zn. This effect, called local action, destroys the Zn. It can be prevented by coating the Zn with mercury.

Remove the Zn electrode from the solution and wipe off the acid. Put a drop of mercury on the Zn and rub with a clean, dry cloth until the surface of the Zn is bright. See that both sides are coated thoroughly. This process is called amalgamation. 

Place the amalgamated Zn in the acid solution and observe the effects. Record your observations after number 3 in "What I observed". If there is no evidence of any considerable chemical action, the Zn is ready for use.

1. When the Cu plate was in the acid solution, I 

2.

When the Zn plate was in the acid solution, I 

3. When the amalgamated Zn was in the acid solution, I observed:

What determines how much current a cell can deliver? Use a cell of C (carbon) and Zn in a

H2SO4 solution. Connect the C to the + post of an ammeter, and the Zn to the post of the ammeter; read the ammeter when the plates are far apart (4). 

Lift the plate holder until the plates are part way out of the solution and read the ammeter (5). 

Put the plates back in their original position in the solution, and push them closer together; read the ammeter (6).

Rinse the plates and place them in the solution in such a position that a fairly large current flows. Note the ammeter reading for any change in current. Observe that bubbles of hydrogen are forming rapidly on the carbon plate. This is called polarization (7). 

Pour some of the chromic acid solution into the electrolyte near the C plate, and note the ammeter reading. Chromic acid is a depolarizing agent (8).

69. How Are Amperes, Volts, and Ohms Related? 

A real understanding of electric circuits requires a thorough knowledge of the relationship that exists among three quantities: current, voltage, and resistance. This relationship, expressed in Ohm's law, is one of the most important laws in electricity.

70. How Does the Resistance of a Wire Vary With Different Factors? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine how the resistance of a conductor varies with different factors. 

Apparatus. Wires of different materials, lengths, and diameters-Dry cells-Voltmeter-Ammeter- 

Rheostat Micrometer caliper or wire gauge.

How to get ready for this activity. The resistance of any conductor is determined by its length, area of cross-section, and temperature. If all these factors remain constant, the resistance will change if the kind of material changes. In order to determine the effect of a change in any one of these factors, the remaining factors must be kept constant. Since the effect of temperature change cannot be measured easily, the effects of changes in the other three factors only will be measured.

Current is commonly supplied to our homes at 

110 or 115 volts. The voltage applied to each lamp, or other electrical appliance, has this value. The length or diameter of the wire in the filament of a lamp determines its resistance, and hence, the amount of current flowing through the lamp. 

What to do. Connect the wire to be measured in series with one or more dry cells, an ammeter, and rheostat as shown in Figure 140. Connect a voltmeter in parallel with the wire. Adjust the rheostat until the ammeter reading is not greater than 0.5 ampere. Keep the current at this value while any one factor is varied. Read the ammeter and voltmeter and calculate the resistance by Ohm's law, P.D.

71. If You Want the Greatest Amount of Current to Flow Through an Electric Circuit, How Must the Resistances Be Connected?

Purpose. To find the effective resistance of several resistors connected in series, and connected in parallel. 

Apparatus.

How to get ready for this activity. Any device used in an electric circuit offers resistance to the flow of current. Most practical circuits contain several resistances, sometimes grouped in series, and sometimes grouped in parallel. If the applied voltage does not change, the current in the circuit depends upon the method of grouping the resistances. The current is greatest when the effective resistance of the circuit is least.

When your automobile battery is "charged” at the garage, the mechanic must control the amount of current passing through it. This amount is changed as the charging proceeds. The control is often accomplished by using several lamps as resistors, and changing the number of lamps through which the current flows when it is desired to change the current.

72. How Can Resistance Be Measured More Accurately 

Than by the Voltmeter-Ammeter Method?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

IR Izar or- IX

I2br Purpose.

To measure resistance by the balancing method. 

Apparatus.

Wheatstone bridge-Galvanometer- 

Dry cell-Resistance box-The three resistance coils used in Problem 71. 

How to get ready for this activity. Great accuracy cannot be obtained in measuring a resistance by the voltmeter-ammeter method. Much better results can be obtained in what is called a balancing method. Four resistances are so adjusted in a circuit, known as a Wheatstone bridge, that no current flows through one branch of the circuit. 

In determining the weight of an object with a beam balance, the unknown weight is balanced against a known weight. The Wheatstone bridge method of measuring resistance is one of balancing an unknown resistance against a known resistance. I₁ I₂ I, I2 

TRAL SCIENTIFIC 

CO Figure 143 In Figure 143 the known resistance R and the unknown resistance X are connected in the of gaps one arm of the Wheatstone bridge. The galvanometer G is connected with one side between R and X, and with the other side to the sliding contact m on the slide wire of the other arm of the bridge. If m is moved along the wire, a point on the wire will be found such that no current flows through the galvanometer. The bridge is then in balance. 

Since no current flows through the galvanometer, points O and m are at the same potential. Hence, P.D. across R=P.D. across a, and P.D. across X =P.D. across b. Let r be the resistance per unit length in the wire. Then IR=I2ar, and IX= 

Ibr. Dividing the first equation by the second,

It is desirable to have R of such a value as to bring the balancing point relatively near the center of the wire.

This method is particularly useful in measuring a small resistance in which a small error may be large in comparison with the resistance itself. 

What to do. Set up the bridge as shown in Figure 

143. Set the sliding contact m near the middle of the wire. Remove from the resistance box a plug representing, say, 10 ohms. Tap the switch at m gently and note the deflection of the galvanometer. 

Suppose that the needle goes to the right. Remove from the box a plug representing some higher value, say, 100 ohms, and again tap the switch gently.

Suppose that the galvanometer needle goes to the left. This tells us that the resistance X has a value somewhere between 10 ohms and 100 ohms. If the needle is deflected more with the 100 ohms than with the 10 ohms, the conclusion is that 10 ohms are too much. Now adjust R until the deflection of the galvanometer needle is as small as possible. Then move the sliding contact m to the right or left, as the case may be, until on closing the switch, the needle shows no deflection. The bridge is now in balance. Record the resistance R and the lengths a and b. Compute the value of X. 

Obtain a balance twice more using first a value of R slightly larger, and then slightly smaller, than at first. 

By the same procedure obtain three measurements for each of the other two coils.

Connect coils A and B in series and determine their effective resistance. Compare this value with their sum. 

Connect coils A, B, and C in series and determine their effective resistance. Compare this value with their sum.

Connect coils A and B in parallel and determine their effective resistance. Compare this value with the computed value obtained from this equation. 1 1 1 R R₁ R₂ Connect coils A, B, and C in parallel and determine their effective resistance. Compare this value with the computed value obtained from this equation. 1 1 1 + + R R₁ R₂ R3 -195-

73. What Do You Pay for When You Buy 

"Electrical Service"?

A current of electricity is used in the lamps of your home to produce light. Light is a form of energy. 

Electricity may be used also to produce heat in an electric iron. Heat is a form of energy. Electricity may be used to produce mechanical energy by driving the motor in your electric fan. It is evident, then, that energy can be obtained from an electric current. 

When you pay your electric bill you pay for the total amount of energy that has been used in all the electrical appliances in your home in a certain period of time. 

At some times you use energy at a faster rate than you do at other times. The meter, which measures the total quantity of energy used, must be able to multiply the rate at which energy is used by the time it is used. 

 Can you buy electricity? Do you pay for current? Do you pay for voltage? What is power? How is power measured? How is electrical energy measured? Does the watt, as used in electricity, have any relation to the watt as used in mechanics?

74. How Can You Use an Electric Current to Obtain Heat or Light? 

Whenever an electric current flows through a conductor, heat is produced. This heat can be put to practical use, as in electric heaters or lamps, or the energy may be lost, and thus reduce the efficiency of the appliance in which the heat is produced. Accidents sometimes occur because of overheating of parts of the electric circuit. Our homes are protected from too great heating effect of the current by causing a certain part of the circuit to melt when the current reaches a certain value. 

 What is a short circuit? Can you connect a 100-watt lamp in the same circuit with an electric stove? What is meant by a mark of 15 on a fuse plug? Current, resistance, time-how do these factors affect the quantity of heat produced?

75. How Is It Possible for an Electrical Engineer to Devise an Electric Iron 

That Gives Neither Too Much nor Too Little Heat?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To measure the heating effect of an electric current.

Apparatus. Electric calorimeter-Voltmeter- 

Ammeter- Rheostat - Switch - Eight dry cells -Thermometer-Beam balance-Set of weights- Watch. 

How to get ready for this activity. Common uses of an electric current are those of driving motors and producing light in lamps. Another use of an electric current, which is coming into more general use, is that of heating.

In commercial practice the heating effect of an electric current has been found most convenient in such uses as maintaining a constant temperature in a part of a radio transmitter, producing high temperature for welding metals, and melting iron in some processes of the manufacture of steel. 

More familiar uses of an electric current for heating purposes are found in the home. An electric heater is often used to warm a room when the furnace fails to supply adequate heat. In recent years the heating effect of an electric current has been used more and more to cook our food. The heating element in a kitchen stove or in an electric iron is a conductor which offers resistance to the flow of current through it. The larger the resistance or the larger the current, the greater the amount of heat developed each second.

The engineer, who designs 'electrical heating equipment, must be able to determine how rapidly heat is given out by a conductor. You can do this easily with a simple form of electric calorimeter, a diagram of which is shown in Figure 145. 

Figure 145

The calorimeter consists of a nickel-plated copper cup into which a heating unit is inserted. The heating wire of this unit consists of an insulated coil with a resistance of about 5 ohms. A sufficient number of cells should be placed in series so as to furnish a current of from 1.5 to 2 amperes.

The heat developed by the current in a given time is expressed by the equation, H=0.24 EIt, in which 

H is in calories, E is the voltage applied to the terminals of the calorimeter, I is the current in amperes, and t the time in seconds. In order to measure Hit is necessary to measure E, I, and t. The voltage is measured by a voltmeter, the current by an ammeter, and the time by a watch. 

The quantity of heat obtained by this method can be checked by computing the heat required to produce this temperature change in the water and calorimeter. This quantity of heat is given by the equation, H₁=mst+m₁s₁t, in which m is the mass of the liquid in grams, s the specific heat of the liquid, m₁ the mass of the calorimeter cup, s₁ the specific heat of the calorimeter cup, and t the temperature change of the water and calorimeter. 

What to do. Arrange the circuit as shown in 

Figure 145 with the battery, switch, rheostat, ammeter, and heating coil connected in series, and the voltmeter connected in parallel with the coil. Close the switch S and adjust the rheostat until the current is between 1.5 and 2 amperes. Now open the switch and proceed with the other measurements. 

Weigh the calorimeter cup, heating coil, and stirring rod, and call their total mass m₁. Fill the cup about three-fourths full of water and weigh again. From these measurements find the mass m of the water. Determine the temperature of the room. 

Start the experiment with the temperature of the water in the calorimeter about 10 degrees below room temperature and end with the final temperature of the water about 10 degrees above room temperature.'

With the switch open insert the coil and thermometer in the water. Record the temperature of the water. Close the switch, record the time, and the reading of the voltmeter. Note the reading of the ammeter and hold the current as nearly constant as possible by adjusting the rheostat. Allow the current to flow until the temperature of the water is about as much above room temperature as it was below at the start, stirring the water occasionally by moving the stirring rod up and down. Record the current. When the switch is opened, record the time and temperature. Compute the heat produced by using the formula H=0.24 EIt.

77. How Can You Use an Electric Current to Produce a Chemical Change in a Substance? 

Chemical change occurring in a cell produces an electric current. On the other hand, an electric current can produce a chemical change. A current is used to extract certain metals from their ores and to plate tableware with silver. The production of current by chemical change, and the production of chemical change by a current are illustrated in the discharging and charging of the storage battery in an automobile. 

 How was the bumper of your car plated with hard, brilliant chromium? How can water be decomposed into hydrogen and oxygen?

78. How Can You Make a Storage Cell? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To make an experimental study of the storage cell. 

Apparatus. Small battery jar-Two strips of lead, about 3 inches wide and 5 or 6 inches long fastened to a strip of wood or other support-Dilute solution of sulfuric acid (1 part acid to 10 parts water by volume)-Electric bell-3 dry cells-Electrodes from a commercial cell. 

How to get ready for this activity. A storage battery is used to start your automobile and to operate the lights. You may not know, however, that, while a storage battery appears to be a somewhat complicated device, it is really very simple so far as the fundamental principles are concerned. A storage cell is simply a voltaic cell, which possesses the peculiar property that enables it to be used alternately as a voltaic cell, and as an electrolytic cell; that is, it can be charged and discharged over and over again. 

A storage cell is an electrolytic cell in which one of the electrodes (anode) becomes highly oxidized. 

In the lead storage cell the anode becomes oxidized by the free oxygen liberated by the decomposition of the sulfuric acid; the cathode is reduced by the free hydrogen. This means that oxygen is removed from the cathode and deposited on the anode. When the cell is charged we have a cell, one electrode of which is lead oxide, and the other more or less free metallic lead. 

In working with a storage cell or battery be extremely careful not to get any of the acid solution on your clothing. Also, never allow any metallic instrument, such as a screw driver, to come into contact with the electrolyte. Iron, or any metal other than lead, dissolved in the electrolyte, will ruin the cell. 

As the current passes through the electrolytic cell, observe the gas at each electrode (2 and 3). After the cell has charged for about 5 minutes, disconnect the charging battery, and connect the bell to the storage cell again (4 and 5). Disconnect the bell and charge the battery again for about 5 minutes. Remove the electrodes from the cell and examine the color of each (6 and 7). 1 Figure 155 

The commercial storage cell. If the electrodes of a commercial lead storage cell (Figure 155) are available examine them with respect to color (8), size (9) distance apart (10), number of plates for each electrode (11).

79. How Car. You Use a Magnetic Field to Produce an Electric Current? 

The electricity that produces light in your home and runs the motor in your electric fan was produced by moving a magnetic field across a conductor, or by moving a conductor across a magnetic field. 

You are able to have and use your electrical equipment largely because of the wonderful discoveries of two great men Oersted and Faraday. Oersted discovered that an electric current produces a magnetic field about a conductor. Faraday discovered that a changing magnetic field can produce a current. 

How is the electricity that you use in your home produced? In what direction does an induced current flow?

Topics to read about. Induced voltage-Induced current-Size and direction of induced voltage and current—Lenz's law-Self-induction-Eddy currents. 

How can you use a magnet to induce a voltage? Wind half the length of about 20 ft. of small, insulated, copper wire into a coil 1 in. or 2 in. in diameter. Support the coil in a vertical position, and connect the free ends to a sensitive galvanometer. Thrust the N pole of a magnet into the coil (Figure 157).

In what direction does an induced current flow? Lenz's law states that if a current is induced by moving a magnetic field across a conductor, or by moving a conductor across a magnetic field, the direction of the induced current is such as to oppose the motion. 

12. If a current is induced in a coil of wire when an 

N pole approaches it, the side of the coil facing the 

N pole is an pole. 

13. If an S pole approaches a coil of wire, the side facing the S pole is an pole. 

Lines of force with the same direction, repel one another. If a straight wire cuts a stationary magnetic field, lines of force around the wire, due to the induced current, will point with the stationary field on one side of the wire, and will point against the field on the other side of the wire. 

14. Will these lines of force point with the stationary field in front of or behind the moving wire? -215—

If the wire is grasped with the right hand with the fingers in front of the moving wire pointing in the same direction as the stationary lines of force, the thumb will point in the direction of flow of current (the current is assumed to flow from positive to negative). How does an alternating current produce "brakes" which hinder its flow? If a direct current (D.C.) flowing in a coil of wire increases, the lines of force surrounding the coil increase. The "new additional lines" must cut across the coil as they go to their places around the coil. This cutting across the coil induces a voltage. Why? Likewise, if the current decreases, the lines of force that surround the coil decrease. As these lines "recede," they must cut across the coil and induce a voltage. 

19. Does this induced voltage help or hinder the change in current?

23. Why does an alternating current require a larger voltage than is required by a steady, direct current to produce a given current in a coil of wire?

How great a voltage will you get when you induce a current? If lines of force move back and forth across a coil of wire, the size of the induced voltage depends upon the number of lines of force, the number of turns of wire, and how fast the lines of force move across the coil. 

24. The greater the number of lines of force, the the induced voltage. 

25. The greater the number of turns of wire, the the induced voltage. 

26. The faster the lines of force move across the coil the the induced voltage.

If lines of force are cut at the rate of 100,000,000 per sec., one volt is induced.

81. What Is the Difference Between a Generator That Produces an Alternating Current and One That Produces a Direct Current? 

In a generator a coil of wire rotates in a magnetic field at uniform speed, thus inducing a voltage which alternates in a regular manner. The current can be sent out from the generator as alternating current or it can be changed to direct current before it leaves the generator. 

 Why does the voltage or current alternate? How is alternating current changed to direct current in the generator, or at the place where it is used?

Topics to read about. Construction of a gen erator-How alternating current is changed to direct current-How alternating or direct current changes as it flows in the circuit. 

How is alternating current produced in a generator? If a conductor moves with uniform speed in a circular path, both the direction and rate of cutting lines of force are continually changing.

82. How Does Electricity Operate the Motor in Your Electric Fan? 

An electric motor is a device for converting electric energy into mechanical energy. This is done by sending an electric current through an armature in a magnetic field, which makes the armature spin. Some motors are devised for operating on direct current; some are devised for operating on alternating current. 

 How are magnetic fields used to make the armature of a motor rotate? Why is a commutator necessary on a 

D.C. motor? How can a motor operate on A.C.? 

How can a motor produce voltage?

Topics to read about. Construction of a motor -Why the armature of a motor rotates-Purpose of a commutator in a motor-Counter voltage in a motor-Efficiency of a motor. 

What makes the armature of a D.C. motor rotate? The operation of a motor depends upon forces between magnetic lines of force. 

1. Do lines of force with the same direction attract or repel one another? 

2. Do lines of force with opposite directions attract or repel one another?

In a motor, as in a generator, an armature rotates in a magnetic field. In Figure 167 the two sides of one turn of wire of the armature are represented by A and B. How can an armature be made to rotate continuously? When the loop of wire of Figure 167 has made a half rotation, wires A and B have exchanged positions. In order that the directions of the lines of force about these wires be such as to cause the rotation to continue in the same direction, the direction of the current in the loop of wire must be reversed.

83. Why Can Some Motors Use Either Alternating Current or Direct Current? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To study the factors that affect the speed and direction of rotation of the armature of a motor. 

Apparatus. St. Louis motor-Dry cells-Alter nating current source "Toy" transformer-Wire— 

Switch-Rheostat 

How to get ready for this activity. The armature of the St. Louis motor is in the form of a cylin-. drical coil. When a current is sent through this armature, it becomes an electromagnet with an N pole at one end and an S pole at the other end. If an S pole of sufficient strength is brought near the N pole of the armature, the force of attraction causes the armature to rotate in such direction as to bring the two poles closer together. If an N pole of the same strength is brought near the N pole of the armature, the force of repulsion causes the armature to rotate in the opposite direction. The armature tends to rotate until the unlike poles causing rotation are as close together as possible. If the commutator then reverses the direction of the current through the armature at this position, repulsion occurs and the armature continues to rotate through another half cycle.

The magnetic field can be provided by means of a permanent magnet or by an electromagnet. The current through the armature and the field magnet may be direct or alternating. 

How does a motor operate on direct current? 

Remove the bar magnets of Figure 168. Then connect one or two dry cells in series with a switch and the binding posts of the motor. Place a compass needle near one end of the armature and close the switch. Commutator Brushes Armature

85. Why Can You Hear Better in Some Movie Theaters Than You Can in Others? 

When an architect plans a movie theater or a large auditorium, he gives particular attention to the acoustic properties. Sound may be heard not only by waves traveling directly from the loud-speaker but also by waves which are reflected from different parts of the room. Some of the reflected waves improve the hearing at certain places in the theater while others interfere with good hearing. A movie theater or auditorium must be designed to help in the reflection of desired waves and to suppress undesired waves. 

 How are sound waves reflected? What is an echo? Why can an echo be heard at some times and not at others? What happens to sound when reflected waves combine with one another and with direct waves? 

What happens to the direction of sound waves when they pass into another medium? How can reflection of sound be prevented?

How are sound waves reflected? If sound waves strike a surface of different density than that of the medium through which they are traveling, some of the energy is reflected. 

1. The direction of the reflected waves depends upon the at which the waves strike the surface. 

2. If the direction of the incident waves makes an angle of 90° with the surface, the direction of the reflected waves make an angle of with the surface. degrees

How can a column of air be made to vibrate?

One end of a glass tube is placed in a jar of water and a tuning fork made to vibrate at the open end of the tube (Figure 173A). Such a tube is called a closed tube. If the length of the air column in the 'tube is changed by raising or lowering the tube in the water, the sound of the fork is greatly in- 

Icreased for a certain length of the air column, because the air is vibrating. The air is vibrating in resonance with the fork.

If the tube is removed from the water so that now both ends are open, resonance is found to occur for a different length. If both ends are open the tube is called an open tube. The length of the tube can be made adjustable by placing a larger tube over it and moving one tube back and forth (Figure 173B).

87. How Are Musical Sounds Produced? 

In music we usually hear sounds from several sources at the same time. To be musical, each sound must meet certain conditions. If the result of two or more musical sounds heard at the same time is to be pleasing, only certain kinds of sounds can be combined.

88. How Can the Same String Produce Tones of Different Pitch and Different Quality? 

A stringed musical instrument must produce a wide range of tones. The strings may be made of wire, as those of a piano, or they may be made of other material, as those of a violin. 89. What Determines the Pitch You Get From a Violin String?

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine how the frequency of a vibrating string depends upon its length, tension, diameter, and mass per unit length. 

Apparatus. Sonometer with sets of wires-Set of weights-Tuning forks-Micrometer caliper or wire gauge.

How to get ready for this activity. When a violinist tunes his violin, he adjusts the tension screws or pegs to obtain the correct pitch. When playing his instrument, he moves his finger along a string to use a different length for each tone that the string produces. Furthermore, some of the strings on a violin or piano, or any other stringed instrument, are heavier than the others.

The pitch of a vibrating string is determined by its frequency of vibration. Hence, the pitch is controlled by the length, tension, diameter, and mass per unit length.

In order to determine the effect of one factor, it must be varied while the others are held constant. 

By using a sonometer (Figure 176) each factor can be varied, one at a time.

Figure 176 If several pupils are performing this experiment in the room at the same time, there may be so much interference of sounds that it will be difficult to get satisfactory results. If the tones are determined by ear, it will be more satisfactory if only small groups perform their experiments at the same time. 

How does the length of a string affect its pitch? Place two wires of the same material and diameter on the sonometer and add the same amount of weight to each weight holder. Call one wire A and the other B. Pluck A and note the tone. Place a bridge under B and move it along until the tone of B when plucked is an octave higher than that of A. Pluck the two wires repeatedly until the ear is satisfied that one tone is an octave higher than the other. 

If you do not recognize tones, the frequencies of the wires can be found by means of standard tuning forks. Change the tension on A until its pitch seems to be nearly the same as that of a C tuning fork (256 vibrations per sec.). Place a small paper rider on A and move the bridge slowly until the rider is thrown off when the stem of the vibrating fork is placed on the sonometer. The frequency of the wire is then the same as that of the fork. 

In a similar manner find the position of the bridge under B for a C¹ fork (512 vibrations per sec.). 

Measure the length of each string and compare the ratio of their lengths with the ratio of their frequencies. 

Repeat the experiment using two new values for the length of A.

How does the tension of a string affect its pitch? Place one wire on the sonometer and change the tension until its pitch is the same as that of a standard tuning fork. Read the value of the tension. 

Change the tension until the pitch of the wire is the same as that of some other tuning fork. Read this tension.

How does the diameter of a string affect its pitch? Place two wires of the same material and length, but of different diameters on the sonometer, and add the same amount of weight to each one. 

Change the length of the larger wire until its pitch is the same as that of a standard tuning fork. Call this frequency 1. Measure this length. Change the length of the smaller wire until its frequency is the same as that of the larger wire. Measure the length of the smaller wire. From the relation of the frequency and length of a wire, compute the frequency that the smaller wire would have if its length were the same as that of the larger wire. Call this frequency 2. 

With the micrometer caliper or wire gauge measure the diameter of each wire. Compare the diameters with the ratio of the frequencies.

90. Why Are There No Keys on a Trombone? 

A pipe organ has a large number of pipes, each of which is capable of producing one tone. For each tone there is a tube of different length. However, a player of a trombone or of some other orchestral wind instrument can produce many tones with but a single pipe. Wind instruments of one kind are not equally good in producing quality of tone. Even on one instrument one player may be able to produce tones of much better quality than can be produced by another player.

How is sound produced in a pipe? Sound is produced in a pipe by the vibration of the air column in it. This air can be made to vibrate in three ways: by blowing across the sharp edge of a mouthpiece, by blowing through a narrow slit, and by vibrating a reed attached to the mouthpiece. 91. How Did the Designer of Your Church Pipe Organ Know How Long to Make Each Pipe? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the wave length of the tone of a tuning fork by the resonance method. 

Apparatus. Glass tube, 1 meter long and 4 cm in diameter-Three tuning forks with frequencies of 

256, 384, and 512 vibrations per sec., respectively 

Ring stand- Clamps - Thermometer Card board tube - Rubber tubing - Can to slide along rod of ring stand. 

In an open pipe the wave travels the length of the pipe and back while the fork makes one vibration. Hence, the length of the pipe is one-half wave length. The second resonant length is one wave length, the third is three-halves wave length, etc. (Figure 180). 2 wave length 

Ist point of resonance Full wave length 

2nd point of resonance 

3rd point of resonance 514 3/1 wave length 1/4 length wave 

Ist point of resonance wave length 

2nd point of resonance 

3rd point of resonance 

Figure 179

Open pipe 

Figure 180

How to get ready for this activity. The essential tone-producing elements of a pipe organ consist of a series of resonance tubes, the lengths of which are selected to give the pitch, the range, and the tone qualities required. Some of the pipes are open and some are closed.

If a tuning fork is vibrating at the open end of a pipe (open or closed), the air in the pipe is set into vibration. If the length of the tube is such as to cause the wave reflected from the opposite end of the pipe to be in phase with that sent out directly from the fork, the intensity of the sound is greatly increased. The fork and the pipe are then said to be in resonance. Since both sounds are produced by the same source, they have the same frequency and wave length.

In the closed pipe the wave travels from the fork to the closed end of the pipe and back to the fork while the fork makes half a vibration, that is, the wave travels one-half wave length. Hence, the length of the tube is one-quarter wave length. If the pipe is made one-half wave length longer, the wave can travel one wave length farther while the fork makes one more vibration. Thus, the second resonant length is three-fourths wave length. Other resonant lengths can be found at the odd-numbered quarter wave lengths (Figure 181). Water

Figure 181 

What to do with the closed tube. Support the closed tube and fork as shown in Figure 179 with the can attached to the tube by means of a rubber tube. (If the can is not available, it can be replaced by a second glass tube. Fit a one-hole rubber stopper in the bottom of each tube and place a piece of short, glass tubing in each stopper. Join the glass tubing by the rubber tubing.) Fill the tube and can with water until the water level is within a few centimeters of the top of the tube. Strike the fork with a soft mallet and hold it at the top of the tube. Lower the water slowly until resonance (maximum intensity) is found. Measure the distance from the open end of the tube to the water level. 

Start the fork again and lower the water until a second resonant length is found. Subtract the first length from the second to obtain a half wave length of the tone of the tuning fork. 

Repeat the experiment with each of the other tuning forks.

Suspend a thermometer in the tube and determine the temperature of the air column. From the speed of sound at this temperature, determine the wave length of the sound from each fork, and compare this value with that obtained from the tube. -245-

How did the designer of your church pipe organ know how long to make each pipe? -246-

1. Play a tune on a siren. The disk should be rotated at a fairly constant speed. With a little practice it is possible to play "America" by blowing a stream of air successively into the holes that correspond to the proper notes. 

2. Make a xylophone. Take a row of eight bottles or tall glasses of the same size and add water successively to different depths in the bottles so that when a given bottle is struck, it will sound a given note. To do this the operator must be able to recognize the notes of the diatonic scale, do, re, mi, etc. A xylophone also can be made by taking thin strips of wood or metal of uniform width and of such lengths that when they are struck the notes of the diatonic scale are produced. It will take some experimentation to cut strips of the right length. 

3. A singing flame. Make and demonstrate a singing flame apparatus.

92. How Can You Make a Camera Without a Lens? 

The sun is the source of most of our light. This light, traveling 93,000,000 miles, reaches the earth in 8.3 minutes. In most of this long, fast journey the sun's light travels through "empty space." The side of the earth facing the sun is well illuminated, but the opposite side is in shadow. This condition is caused by the particular way in which light travels. 

What is light? How does light get from the sun to us? How fast does light "zip" to us through "empty space"? How does light enable you to see things?

How are shadows formed? An opaque object blocks off all light falling upon it. Therefore, since light travels in straight lines from a source, the region behind the object cannot be illuminated by the source. How can an image be formed through a pinhole? If light passes through a very small opening, a clear image of the source can be formed on a screen (Figure 184). This is possible only because light travels in straight lines. If the image is to be clear, any two rays of light leaving a point of the source and passing through the pin-hole, must fall at almost the same place on the screen.

93. How Can You Choose Reading Lamps That Treat Your Eyes Kindly? 

Too little light is a waste of eyesight. Too much light in a room is waste of energy. For certain types of work too much light is almost as bad as too little light both for effectiveness of work and for conservation of eye-sight.

How is brightness of illumination related to the brightness of the source and the distance from the source? What illumination is required for different activities? How are brightness of illumination and brightness of a source measured?

Purpose. To measure the candle power of a source of light. 

Apparatus. Student optical bench with photometer (Figure 186)-Candles-Kerosene or alcohol lamp-Electric lamp.

ing an unknown source the photometer is illuminated on one side by a source of known candle power at a known distance, and on the other side by the source of unknown candle power at a known distance. The illumination on the paper varies inversely as the square of the distance from the paper to the source. 

If the two sources produce equal illumination on the paper, their candle powers are proportional to the squares of their distances from the paper. Let A be the source of known candle power, X the source of unknown candle power, d, the distance from A to the photometer, and d, the distance from X to the photometer. Then— 

Figure 186 

How to get ready for this activity. A photometer consists of a cardboard or other support with a piece of waxed, or greased paper at its center. If the waxed paper is equally illuminated on both sides, it will appear to have the same color on each side. But if the two sides are not equally illuminated, one side will appear to be darker than the other. A mirror placed on each side of the cardboard at an angle to it, and below the waxed paper, permits one person to observe both sides of the waxed paper. In measur- d2 d2 

What to do. Place one candle in the holder at one end of the optical bench and two candles in the holder at the other end of the bench. Move the photometer back and forth until the spot on the paper is equally illuminated on both sides. Measure the distance from the waxed paper to the light source at each end. Displace the photometer and make two more trials for the same light source. From the average of the three trials compute the candle power of the two candles. 

Replace the candles with an electric lamp and repeat the measurements. 

Replace the electric lamp with the kerosene or alcohol lamp (or any other source) and repeat the measurements.

95. How Does a Mirror Enable You to See Yourself?

When light falls on any surface, some of it is reflected. In order to be a good mirror, the surface must reflect most of the light falling on it and must reflect this light in particular directions. Some mirrors reflect light in such manner that images can be formed on a screen.

96. How Are Images Formed by Mirrors? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITIES 

Purpose. To determine the focal length of a spherical mirror; to determine what kinds of images are formed by spherical mirrors. 

Apparatus. Concave spherical mirror of 30 cm to 40 cm focal length-Convex spherical mirror- 

Mirror holder-Meter stick-Candle. 

How to get ready for this activity. For the purpose of looking into the heavens, large curved mirrors are sometimes used in giant telescopes. The most recent mirror of this kind at Mt. Wilson, California, has a diameter of 200 in. All the light from a star, falling on the large area of this mirror, is brought to a focus on a small area thus giving a great intensity of light. Light rays coming from a distant star are parallel, thus causing the image to be formed at the principal focus. It often happens, however, that the source is so close to the mirror that the light rays from it are not parallel. The location of the image and its nature are determined by the distance of the source from the mirror. The properties of the image can be determined graphically if the focal length of the mirror and the distance of the object from the mirror are known. 

If the object is not at an infinite distance from the mirror, the focal length of the mirror can be found from the equation, f= dodi do+di in which ƒ is the focal length of the mirror, d, is the distance of the object from the vertex of the mirror, and d; is the distance of the image from the vertex of the mirror.

A virtual image can be formed with both a concave mirror and a convex mirror. The distance of a virtual image from the mirror cannot be measured.

97. How Is It Possible for a Diver to Look Up Toward the Sky and See the Bottom of a Lake?

Light travels more rapidly in air than it does in water. Consequently, the direction of light changes when it passes from air into water or from water into air. If we observe an object through a transparent medium, we are likely to believe that its position is different from its true position, since our experience tells us that light travels in a straight line in a uniform medium.
