98. In Your Laboratory How Can You Measure the Speed of Light in Glass? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the index of refraction of a given sample of glass. 

Apparatus. Glass plate with parallel edges-Pro tractor-Paper-Thumb tacks-Smooth board. 

How to get ready for this activity. Light travels at a speed of approximately 186,000 miles per sec. in a vacuum or in air. The measurement of such speed requires great experimental skill because of the difficulty in obtaining a sufficiently large time interval for accurate measurement. The most accurate measurement of the speed of light was done by Michelson. In his experiment he caused a beam of light to travel the round trip between two points located approximately 22 miles apart. 

Light travels less rapidly in other materials than it does in air. Because of this fact a beam of light is refracted upon passing at an oblique angle from air into another medium. We can make use of this bending of light to measure its speed in the medium without making any time measurements. This is done by comparing the angles of incidence and refraction. If we divide the speed of light in air by its speed in some other medium, the quotient is called the index of refraction of the medium. 

In a right-angled triangle the ratio of the side opposite an acute angle to the hypotenuse is defined as the sine of the angle. That the index of refraction of a medium is the ratio of the sine of the angle of incidence to the sine of the angle of refraction can be shown as follows: sine i AO/OH AO S sine r CH/OH CH S1 the index of refraction. OB and OF are radii of a circle drawn about O. Thention. sine i BE/OB BE 

sine r FD/OF FD

99. How Are Images Formed by Lenses? 

LABORATORY ACTIVITY 

Purpose. To determine the focal length of a convex lens; to study the images formed by lenses. 

Apparatus. Optical bench with necessary supports-Convex lens of 30 cm to 40 cm focal length— Concave lens-Pieces of cardboard-Candle or electric lamp. 

How to get ready for this activity. When you take a picture with a camera, light rays passing through the front of the camera must come to a focus on the film. An image is formed at a point on the film where light rays leaving a point on the object come together again. A lens in the front of the camera causes the light rays to converge on the film to form an image. The distance between the lens and the film must be changed as the distance between the object and camera is changed if a clear image is to be formed on the film. The size of the image depends upon the distance of the object from the camera. 

If the object is at an infinite distance from the lens, the image is formed at the principal focus. Usually the object is relatively close to the lens. The image is then formed at some point other than the principal focus. Its location, size, and nature are determined by the distance between the object and lens. The focal length of a lens can be found by the equation, dodi do+di in which ƒ is the focal length of the lens, d, is the distance between the object and the center of the lens, and d; is the distance between the image and the center of the lens.

A virtual image can be formed with both a convex lens and a concave lens. The distance of the virtual image from the lens cannot be measured.

After you have studied each image in the following experiments, compare its characteristics with the diagram showing the formation of the image by that type of lens, and for that particular location of the object.

Measure the distance from the screen to the center of the lens. This is the focal length of the lens, since the source is at an infinite distance. Find the image and image distance for three trials, and find the average image distance.

101. How Does Your Eye Operate 

Like a Camera? 

If you see an object, the lens in your eye forms an image of it in the same manner that a camera lens forms an image. With good eye-sight a clear image is produced. If the lens in the eye does not refract light rays correctly, the image is indistinct, and glasses are necessary.

In using a camera you adjust the lens for different distances of the object from the camera. The eye requires no such adjustment, but is able to produce clear images for near and distant objects. 

The size of the image in the eye changes if the distance of the object changes. Yet we have little difficulty in judging the size of an object. 

Things to think about. How do your eyes form images? How may eye defects be corrected? Are motion pictures really moving when you see them?

102. What Causes the Colors in Your New Coat? 

Sunlight is made up of many colors. When sunlight is reflected from different objects, they may have different colors. Grass is green in sunlight because it absorbs all the color except green from the white sunlight, and reflects the green. 

If beams of light of different colors are mixed, new colors result. An artist knows how to mix pigments to obtain the colors he desires. 

Things to think about. How are colors produced? What are primary colors? What are primary pigments? What are complementary colors and complementary pigments? How does mixing colors differ from mixing pigments? What causes the rainbow? What causes the changing colors on a wild duck's neck?

Topics to read about. Nature of color-Disper sion of light-The solar spectrum-Mixing colors and pigments-Chromatic aberration-Kinds of spectra-Color effects due to interference. 

How does a prism break up light into colors?

If a prism is held in sunlight as shown in Figure 220, a band of colors, known as the solar spectrum, can be thrown on the wall. These colors are all the "colors of the rainbow," ranging from violet on one end to red on the other end.

The frequency of visible light varies from approximately 395,000,000,000,000 to 750,000,000,000,000 per sec., or the wave length from approximately 

0.000076 cm to 0.00004 cm.

If beams of colored light are mixed, the effect of one adds to the effects of the others. But if pigments are mixed, the effect of one subtracts from the effects of the others. The three primary pigments represented in Figure 222 are the complements of the three primary colors of Figure 221. 

The colors and their complements are placed in the same relative positions in the two figures. Thus, the pairs of complementary, colors are red and peacock blue, green and crimson, bluish-violet and yellow. If the pigments of Figure 222 are mixed, different colors result.

103. How Are Radio Messages Transmitted From a Broadcasting Station to Your Home?

Radio messages are transmitted by waves, which travel with the speed of light through space. No material medium, such as air, is needed for the transmission of these waves. All waves, such as light and radio, which carry radiant energy, are called electromagnetic waves. 

Since radio waves transmit radiant energy, no sound is actually sent from the broadcasting station to your home. Radio waves are so controlled that the sounds made at the microphone of the broadcasting station can be reproduced in your radio receiving set in a manner somewhat similar to that by which the voice of your friend at the transmitter of his telephone is reproduced in the receiver of your telephone. The radio waves between the sending and receiving sets correspond to the pulses of electric current in the wire joining the two telephones.

Just as light has a range of frequencies, radio waves have a range of frequencies, but much greater. 

The tuning dial on your radio works over the range of frequencies commonly used for general broadcasting, but radio waves are often used with much higher and much lower frequencies. Stations broadcasting on the higher frequencies can be heard farther than stations with the same power broadcasting on the lower frequencies. Radio communication between different countries is done on high frequency, that is, short wave length. 

Things to think about. What is the difference between radio waves and light waves? What are damped waves? What are undamped waves? What are modulated waves? What is the range of frequencies of electromagnetic waves? What are the frequencies and wave lengths received by your radio?

How do frequency and wave length vary in your receiving set? There is a definite relation between the velocity, frequency, and wave length of all electromagnetic waves. This relation is expressed by the equation v=nl. In metric units v=300,000,000 meters per sec. Radio frequency is usually expressed in kilocycles per sec. A cycle is one vibration. For example, a pendulum makes one vibration, or one cycle, when it swings from one side to the other and back again.

How does the amplitude of radio waves vary?

If a spark occurs between the terminals of a Leyden jar or an induction coil, it really jumps back and forth several times, the amount of energy decreasing on each trip between the terminals, until it finally dies out. Thus, there is sent out a train of waves with decreasing amplitude, called damped waves. 

10. Make a drawing here of a train of damped waves. 

-DRAWING- 

-281-

In a radio broadcasting station, the vibrations are maintained undamped by adding energy at the proper times in each cycle. Thus, the wave train sent out has a constant amplitude; that is, the waves are undamped. These waves are called carrier waves. 

11. Make a drawing here of an undamped wave train. 

-DRAWING- 16. What part of a radio wave is modulated? 

The "humps" and "hollows" of a modulated wave train correspond to the amplitude of the sound waves at the microphone.

104. How Does a Radio Work? 

We use our radio receiving sets to pick up the radio messages broadcast from the transmitting station. A radio receiving set must be designed to pick up energy from the modulated waves that cross the antenna, and to separate the audio frequency part from the combination of audio frequency and radio frequency.

When the waves cross the antenna they set up currents in the antenna. By use of the magnetic and electric effects of these currents vacuum tubes operate to separate out the audio frequency, and to amplify the current until it passes through the loudspeaker and we hear the sound. 

Things to think about. What kind of current flows in the antenna? Through what part of the radio does this current flow? What are the parts of a vacuum tube? What is the purpose of each part?

105. What Are X Rays and Radium "Rays"? 

The use of X rays by the doctor to check for broken bones is a familiar procedure. X rays are very penetrating radiations produced by hurling electrified particles at a metal object enclosed. in a vacuum tube. 

Another type of penetrating radiation is produced in nature by a process called radioactivity. In this process, which occurs in but a few elements, a part of an atom experiences a relatively violent explosion, with the result that electrified particles are thrown from the atom, and a very penetrating of radiation is emitted. type 

Things to think about. What effects are produced when electrified particles pass through a vacuum tube? How are X rays produced? What is a radioactive material? How does radioactivity compare with X rays? What kinds of "rays" are emitted by radium?

Topics to read about. Cathode rays-X rays—

What are X rays? If the voltage used to drive the cathode rays through a tube is sufficiently high, X rays will be given out by one of the electrodes.

What are cathode rays? An electrical discharge occurs between the electrodes of a vacuum tube if the potential is sufficiently high. The discharge is a stream of electrically charged particles, which are called cathode rays. 

1. Do the particles leave the positive electrode or the negative electrode?

Air keeps Solids, liquids, gases. Matter is found in three states: solid, liquid, gaseous.

A solid has mass, weight, volume, and shape.

Matter has mass and weight: Any object has mass and weight.

Mass: the quantity of matter in an object.

Weight: the amount of force with which the earth pulls on an object at the surface of the earth. 

Symbol: W. The greater the mass of an object, the greater its weight. If you carry an object up a mountain, its mass remains the same; but, its weight decreases because it is farther from the center of the earth.

The attraction of the earth for one pound of mass is one pound of force.

gas has mass and weight, but no definite shape or definite volume. Gases must be kept in enclosed containers. Gases expand indefinitely. Gases are distributed under certain standards of pressure. 

Measurement of matter. Many properties of matter are determined by accurate measuring.

Gravity is one of the earth's natural forces.

Measurement of force. A force is usually measured with a balance and can be expressed in units of weight, such as the pound or gram weight. 

Pressure: force per unit area. 

Computing pressure. Pressure is computed by dividing the force exerted on some object by the area on which the force acts. Pressure is expressed as pounds per in.2 or gm per cm² or in other units of force and area. Symbol: P.

The pressure at any point in a liquid is equal in all directions. If the pressure were not the same in all directions at any one point, water would rush from a point of higher pressure to a point of lower pressure until the pressures from different directions were equal.

The pressure of a liquid is independent of the shape or size of the container. At any depth in a liquid, 288

the liquid exerts a pressure which is perpendicular to the surface on which it acts. In the three vases, the pressure is the same but the volumes of the liquid are unequal. The area of the base and the height of the liquid are the same in each case.

Buoyancy: An object weighed in a liquid apparently loses weight equal to the weight of the liquid displaced. The apparent loss of weight is caused by the buoyancy of the liquid. The buoyancy of the liquid is equal to the weight of the liquid displaced. 

The apparent loss of weight is added to the total force against the bottom of the container because the height of the liquid is increased. 155 gm

Liquid in connected vessels: A liquid tends to seek the same level in connected vessels or pipes. 

Since pressure is independent of the size or shape of the container, the tubes or vessels need not be the same size. For the same vertical height the pressures will be the same.

Specific gravity: The specific gravity of any material is the ratio of its weight to the weight of an equal volume of water. Example: The specific grav ity of material which has a volume of 2 ft.

and 337 2X62.4 weighs 337 lb. =2.7. 

Transmission of pressure: Pressure applied to a confined liquid is transmitted equally and without loss throughout all parts of the liquid (Pascal's principle). In the hydraulic press a force is applied to the small piston. The load to be lifted is placed on a large piston. Since the pressures are the same, the total force against the large piston will be greater. 

However, the large piston moves a much smaller distance. What is gained in force is lost in distance.

The specific gravity of a material shows its density in comparison with the density of water. If the density is expressed in gm per cc, the density and specific gravity are numerically equal. This is true because the mass of 1 cc of water is taken as 1 gm. 

The specific gravity of a liquid can be measured with a hydrometer. The hydrometer must sink deeper in a less dense liquid to displace its own weight. 

Unit 2. Mechanics of Gases 

Properties of gases: Gases have some properties similar to those of liquids, and some properties that are different.

A gas occupies space. Water will not enter a container unless the gas is permitted to escape. Water Water 

Water does

Air. not enter -bottle. Air- Water

A gas has weight. The weight of 1 ft.³ of air at sea level is approximately 1.25 oz. The weight of 

1 liter of air is 1.29 gm. The weight of 1 liter of hydrogen is 0.09 gm.

A gas exerts pressure. The barometer is used to measure the pressure of the air. The pressure of the air is measured by the weight of the column of mercury which it will support. The normal pressure of the air is 14.7 lb. per in.2 or 1033.6 gm per cm².

This pressure is equivalent to the pressure of a column of mercury 29.92 in., or 76 cm, high.

Since gases are free to expand and the pressure changes rapidly, it is necessary to accept a standard condition for measurement of pressure. Sometimes standard pressure is taken as the reading of the 289

barometer at sea level. This is really not a standard because air pressure changes at sea level as well as at any other place. The standard pressure of air is equivalent to the pressure of a column of mercury

The pressure of the air decreases with an increase in altitude. About one-half of the air is within three miles of the earth's surface. Airplanes operate most efficiently at the pressures (altitudes) for which they are designed.

Gases are compressible. Since a gas has no definite volume it is possible to change the volume. 

Liquids are considered to be incompressible. The volume of a given mass of gas decreases with an increase in pressure. According to Boyle's law the volume of a given mass of any gas is inversely proportional to its pressure, provided that the temperature remains the same. or

Low velocity, high pressure 

Moving gas causes changes in pressure. If the speed of a stream of air or water is increased in one direction, the pressure at right angles to the line of direction is decreased (Bernoulli's principle). 

The aspirator, atomizer, carburetor, and Bunsen burner are caused to operate because a stream of air or liquid decreases pressure. Lift is developed in an airplane by having the air travel at greater speed above the wings than it does below the wings. 

This causes a low pressure area above the wings. 

Gases are elastic. If an automobile tire passes over a rock, the air in the tire is compressed at that point. The inertia of the car prevents it from rising instantly. The compressed air pushes the tire out very quickly. Footballs, basketballs, and tennis balls bounce because the air in them is elastic. 

Push of air 

Push of propeller

A gas has inertia. Air tends to stay where it is. 

The propeller of an airplane is pushed forward because of the inertia of the air. It takes considerable force in an electric fan to push the air forward. The fan tends to move backward.

Composition of matter: Matter is composed of small particles called molecules. The molecules of solids, liquids, and gases are in constant motion. 

When many molecules of air are in motion in one direction, they are felt as a wind. Molecules 

Tensile strength: the force required to pull apart a material having a cross-sectional area of one unit. 

Ductility: the property of materials which makes it possible to pull them into fine threads or tubes. 

Example: Glass can be pulled into a fine thread when heated until it is soft.

Forces exerted by molecules: Molecules have forces of attraction for one another.

The force of attraction is greater in solids than in liquids, and greater in liquids than in gases.

Cohesion: the force of attraction of like particles for one another. Example: Your pen point keeps its shape because of cohesion. par- 

Adhesion: the force of attraction of unlike ticles for one another. Example: Chalk sticks to a blackboard.

Malleability: the property which makes it possible to pound a material into a thin layer without breaking it. Example: Gold is the most malleable metal; it is frequently pounded into sheets as thin as 1/1000 of an inch.

Elasticity: the property which enables an object to recover from a distortion such as a bend, twist, or stretch. Example: A football bounces because the air in the ball makes it elastic.

Elastic limit. If molecules of a solid are separated too far, they do not return to their original positions. 

Steel spring

Meter stick Wood Glue Wood 

Hooke's Law. Within the elastic limit, the force causing the distortion of a solid is proportional to the distortion. Example: A spring balance stretches twice as far when a 2-kg force is applied to it as it does for a stretching force of 1 kg. 

The force of cohesion keeps water in the beaker.

The force of adhesion of glue for wood holds the blocks together.

Surface tension: result of the tendency of a liquid to form a film at the surface. This film acts like a stretched, elastic membrane. It causes the surface of a liquid to try to contract to a smaller area. (See the illustration on the following page) 291

Capillarity: the process by which a liquid rises in a tube which it wets and is depressed in a tube which it does not wet. Example: Melted paraffin rises in the wick of a candle. Capillarity is partly due to surface tension.

Safety factor in structures. An engineer must construct a building to provide for the safety factor. 

A building is constructed to hold a greater load than the computed forces on it. Additional loads may be caused by winds, snow, and sudden motions or vibrations.

Units of force: 

Poundal: the force needed to give one pound of mass an acceleration of 1 ft. per sec. per sec. 

Dyne: the force needed to give one gm of mass an acceleration of 1 cm per sec. per sec. Force equals mass times acceleration: F=ma. If m is in gm, and a is in cm per sec. per sec., F is in dynes. 

Newton's Laws of motion: 

First Law of motion. A force must be applied to an object if its condition of rest, its speed, or direction are to be changed. An object at rest tends to remain at rest and an object in motion tends to continue with the same speed in a straight line unless acted upon by some outside force. This is the law of inertia. Example: Your book remains in the same place on your desk unless a force is applied to it. 

Second Law of motion. Change of motion is proportional to the force acting and occurs in the direction of the force. Example: Force is necessary to increase the speed of an automobile, or to turn it from a straight line path. Push 

Car moves in direction of force. Acceleration is proportional to the force.

Direction of motion 

Third law of motion. For every action there is an equal and opposite reaction. Example: When you jump from a chair on a polished floor, the chair moves in the opposite direction with a momentum equal and opposite to your momentum. 292

A force or any other vector quantity can be represented graphically by a straight line. The line represents the point of application, the magnitude, and direction of the force.

Resultant of forces: The resultant of two or more forces is the single force which will produce the same effect as the other forces.

Parallel forces. The resultant of parallel forces acting in the same direction is equal to the sum of the forces. 

Points of application 

Resultant of A and B 

Resultant of two parallel forces in the same direction

Concurrent forces. The resultant of two concurrent forces can be obtained graphically by using a parallelogram. The diagonal of the parallelogram represents the resultant when the two forces are drawn to scale. 

Moment of a force: A moment of a force is the product of the force and the perpendicular distance between the force and the point on which it acts. 

The moment is the turning effect, or torque, about a point.

Equilibrium: An object is in equilibrium when there are no unbalanced forces acting on it.

An object upon which two or more forces are acting is in equilibrium if: 

1. The sum of the forces acting in one direction equals the sum of the forces acting in the opposite direction. 

2. The sum of the clockwise moments equals the sum of the counterclockwise moments. Equilibrant

Equilibrant: The equilibrant is the single force which will balance all the forces acting on an object. 

The equilibrant is equal to and acts in a direction opposite to the resultant. Example: When a team of horses pulls a wagon forward, the force of friction pushing backward provides the equilibrant to the resultant pull of the two horses.

Friction: Friction is the resistance an object offers to another object being moved over it. Two perfectly smooth surfaces in contact would be frictionless. Sliding friction is greater than rolling friction.

The coefficient of sliding friction is equal to the force required to move the object over some surface divided by the force holding the two surfaces together. Friction is decreased by lubrication.

Unit 6. Heat

Nature of heat: Heat is a form of energy. 

Sources of heat: The sun is our principal source of heat energy. Heat is produced artificially by: (1) Converting the chemical energy in fuels to heat energy, (2) Converting electrical energy to heat energy, (3) Rubbing two surfaces against each other (friction).

Temperature: Temperature represents the degree of hotness of an object. According to the kinetic theory of matter the small particles, or molecules, of which matter is composed are in constant motion. 

The greater the speeds of the molecules the higher the temperature.

Measurement of temperature. Temperature is measured with a thermometer. Change in temperature is measured by the expansión or contraction of some form of matter, usually a liquid.

1 gm of water 1 C degree or the quantity of heat set free when 1 gm of water cools 1 C degree. A large calorie (food calorie) is equal to 1000 small calories. A British Thermal Unit is the quantity of heat needed to raise the temperature of 1 lb. of water 1 F degree.

absorbed Specific heat: The specific heat of a material is the ratio between the quantity of heat needed to raise the temperature of any mass of that material any number of degrees, and the quantity of heat needed to raise the temperature of an equal mass of water through the same number of degrees. In the metric system specific heat of any material can be considered as the number of calories needed to raise the temperature of 1 gm of that material 1 C degree.

Measuring specific heat: If two materials are mixed, one material gains as much heat energy as the other loses. This is the law of mixtures. If a heated metal is placed in water, the metal loses as much heat as the water gains. The number of calories gained or lost by any material is equal to its mass times the change in temperature times the specific heat. The specific heat of a material can be measured by the use of the law of mixtures.

Water expands when cooled from 4°C to 0°C, and contracts when heated from 0°C to 4°C. The temperature of the water at the bottom of a deep lake is approximately 4°C when ice is being formed at the top. 20°C 0°C 4°C Water Water Water

Fusion: The melting of a solid is called fusion. 

Heat of fusion. The number of units of heat needed to change a unit mass of any material from the solid to liquid state without any change in temperature. The heat of fusion of ice is 80 calories per gm, or 144 B.T.U. per lb. A substance melts and freezes at the same temperature. When ice melts each gm absorbs 80 calories of heat. The energy absorbed increases the motions of the molecules and causes the molecules to move farther apart. Ice is used for cooling because heat is absorbed from materials around it when it melts. 

When 1 gm of water at 0°C changes to ice at 0°C, 80 calories of heat are released. The motions of the molecules are decreased, and they come closer together.

Measuring heat of fusion. The heat of fusion of ice can be measured by the use of the law of mixtures. 4°C 4°C Expansion of a gas.

Evaporation: Evaporation is a heat absorbing process. Evaporation of a liquid is the changing of the liquid to a vapor at the surface of the liquid. It may occur at any temperature between the freezing and boiling point of the liquid. Heat energy is needed to cause evaporation. Boiling is the changing of a liquid to a vapor throughout all parts of the liquid. The boiling point of water is 100°C when the pressure is 76 cm of mercury. The boiling point of a liquid is lowered by a decrease in the pressure and raised by an increase in pressure. Example: On

Humidity: The quantity of water vapor that the air can hold is dependent upon the temperature. The capacity of the air to hold water vapor is increased by increasing the temperature. Relative humidity refers to the quantity of water vapor the air does hold compared to what it could hold at the same temperature if it were saturated. When the air is saturated with water vapor the relative humidity is 

100 per cent. The quantity of water vapor in the air at any one time is its absolute humidity. It is usually represented as so many gm per m³, or grains per ft.³ 

The dew point is the temperature at which the water vapor in the air begins to condense. If the dew point is below freezing, frost is formed. 

If cold air is brought into a room in winter and heated its relative humidity is lowered. Water vapor must be added to maintain a satisfactory relative humidity. In summer if warm air is brought into a home and cooled, the relative humidity is increased. 

Water vapor must be absorbed if a satisfactory relative humidity is to be maintained. Porous cloth

In a thermos bottle heat loss by convection and conduction is prevented by having a vacuum between the walls of the bottle. Loss by radiation is retarded by silvering the inside surfaces of the two walls.

Mechanical equivalent of heat: When work is converted into heat, it takes 778 ft. lb. of work to produce 1 B.T.U., or 427 gm m to produce 1 calorie.

One lb. of soft coal releases 12,000 B.T.U. of heat energy when burned. Each B.T.U. is equivalent to 

778 ft. lb. of work. The energy in 1 lb. of coal is equivalent to 12,000×778 =9,336,000 ft. lb. of work.

Heat transfer: Heat can be transferred from one place to another by conduction, convection, or radiation. Heat is transferred by conduction through a material by increasing the motions of the molecules by heating one part of the material. Metals are good conductors of heat. Gas engine. In a gasoline engine, the force needed to move the piston is derived from the expansive force of the mixture of air and gasoline when burned at a high enough temperature. Gases expand when heated, according to Charles' Law. 

Intake stroke: The piston moves down, increasing the volume and decreasing the pressure in the cylinder, permitting a mixture of gasoline vapor and air to enter. Intake valve open. Compression stroke:

The piston moves up decreasing the volume of the mixture and increasing the pressure. Both valves closed. Energy stroke: The mixture is ignited and burned at a high temperature. The high temper- Intake Compres sion

Exhaust stroke: The piston moves up and pushes the exhaust gases out through the open exhaust valve.

Unit 7. Magnetism and Charges of Static Electricity 

Force between poles: Like magnetic poles repel each other; unlike magnetic poles attract each other. 

The force of attraction or repulsion is given by the equation F=P₁P₂/d2. If d is in cm and P1 and P2 in magnetic units, F is in dynes. If the distance between two 'magnetic poles is doubled, the force between them is but one-fourth as great. 

Lines of force around a magnet 

Lines of force leave the N pole and enter at the S pole.

Like poles repel

Unlike poles attract

Magnetic materials: The ore, lodestone, has magnetic properties. Steel is used to make permanent magnets; soft iron is used for temporary magnets. 

Theory of magnetization: One magnet can be made from another magnet. Each molecule of iron or steel is assumed to be a magnet. If all the molecules in a bar of iron are arranged to exert their magnetism together, the iron will exhibit magnetic properties. It is assumed that in a magnet the poles of the molecules point in the same direction.

In unmagnetized iron the poles of the molecules have a haphazard arrangement. 

An iron nail rubbed along a magnet becomes magnetized. 

Field of force: A magnet is surrounded with a field of force. The direction of a line of force is the path taken by an independent N pole in moving from the N pole to the S pole of the magnet. A line of force leaves the N pole of a magnet and goes through the air to the S pole, and then back to the N pole through the magnet. A line of force is a closed curve. 

Magnetism of the earth. A compass needle sets itself parallel to the direction of the earth's lines of force. The earth's N magnetic pole is near the south geographic pole. The S magnetic pole is near the north geographic pole. The ends of a compass needle point in the directions of the earth's magnetic poles.

SON 

Compass needle points in the direction of the earth's lines of force. 

Magnetic maps. Maps are made of the earth's magnetic field. It is necessary to map the earth's magnetic lines of force at regular intervals because of changes in the earth's magnetic field. Lines are drawn through points which have the same compass readings (isogonic lines). Lines are drawn through points which have the same readings on the dip needle (isoclinic lines).

Production of electric charge: Electrification can be produced by friction. A hard rubber rod can be electrified by rubbing it with flannel or fur. Glass can be electrified by rubbing it with silk. The hard rubber is said to have a negative charge, and the glass a positive charge. Any electric charge is either positive or negative. 300

If 9 is in electrostatic units and d in cm, F is in dynes. If the distance between two charges is tripled, the force between them is but one-ninth as great. 

Detection and identification of charge: An electroscope is used to detect charge and identify kind of charge. If a charged object is brought near the knob of an uncharged electroscope, the leaves diverge. If an electroscope has a known charge, and the leaves diverge further when a charged object is brought near, the charges on the electroscope and object are the same. If the leaves diverge less, the charges are opposite. Uncharged * *

Theory of electrification: The nature of electricity is explained by the electron theory. All atoms of matter are composed of charged particles. The negatively charged particles are called electrons, and the positively charged particles protons. The electrons may move, or flow. In a neutral atom there are equal numbers of electrons and protons. 

Example: An atom of copper contains 29 electrons and 29 protons. The protons are in the nucleus of the atom. The nucleus also contains uncharged particles called neutrons. 

Storing up charge: A condenser is used to store up electric charges. A condenser is composed of two conductors separated by a non-conductor. The capacity of the condenser is increased by increasing the area of the plates and by decreasing the distance between the plates. The kind of dielectric affects the capacity. A condenser is used in tuning in a station on the radio. Wood -Metal Glass 

Hydrogen atom has I proton and I electron Oxygen atom 8 electrons 8 protons 8 neutrons 8+ 8 ± 

Conduction and induction. One electrified object can be used to electrify another object. 

Objects can be charged by conduction or by induction. If an uncharged object is charged by a charged object by conduction, both objects will have the same charge. If charged by induction, the objects will have opposite charges.

Electrons flow to ground Metal foil Metal Metal -chain foil Ground 

Ground 

Lightning is the discharge of electricity between a cloud and the earth or between clouds. The high potential of the charge in the clouds overcomes the resistance of the air and a discharge is produced. 

A cloud and the earth or two clouds constitute the plates of a condenser. Thunder is caused by violent disturbances of air set up by the lightning discharge. 

Unit 8. Current Electricity and the Effects of the Electric Current 

The voltaic cell: Electrical energy can be produced by chemical action. An electric cell can be made by using two dissimilar metals and a solution which acts on one of the metals. The metals form the electrodes and the solution the electrolyte. The dry cell is a voltaic cell.

Electrical units: The electromotive force produced in the cell is measured in volts (E). The electromotive force of a cell is determined by the materials used in making the cell, and not on the size of the plates or their distance apart. A cell for a flashlight has the same voltage as a large cell. 301

The resistance of the cell and circuit is measured in ohms (R). The current flowing in the circuit is measured in amperes (I). The current delivered by a cell is determined by the size and distance apart of the plates, and by the material between the plates. 

A large cell has less resistance than a small cell. 

Voltmeter 

Carbon Zinc

Solution Cardboard 

Electrons flow from negative to positive.

By convention positive charges are assumed to flow from positive to negative.

Ohm's law: The current flowing in a circuit is equal to the effective electromotive force divided by the effective resistance.

ductors. The resistance of a wire is equal to the potential difference divided by the current flowing through the wire.

If the cells are alike the effective electromotive force is nE where n is the number of cells. When cells are connected in parallel the effective electromotive force is the same as that of one cell.

Potential difference: the difference in potential between any two points in a circuit when current is flowing. Symbol: P.D. Electromotive force and potential difference are measured with a voltmeter. 

The voltmeter is a high resistance instrument and is connected in parallel with the resistance across which the P.D. is measured. The ammeter is a low resistance instrument and is connected in series in the circuit. Cell

Combinations of resistances: Any combination of resistances in a circuit can be replaced by a single resistance that will allow the same current to flow. 

This single resistance is called the effective resistance. 

Series. Resistances are connected in series if the current flows through them in consecutive order.

In a series circuit all of the current flows through all parts of the circuit. The effective resistance of conductors connected in series is the sum of the separate resistances.

Magnetic effect of the current: A wire conducting a current has a magnetic field around it. 

Direction of current 

Right hand rule Compass above wire Current coming from the page 

Straight conductor. The lines of force around a conductor are in the form of concentric circles. 

The right hand rule gives the relation between the direction of the current and the direction of the field. The direction of the lines of force around a wire can be used to determine the direction of the flow of current.

Coil. The lines of force around a wire are concentrated by winding the wire in a loop or coil.

The lines of force in a coil will magnetize an iron bar placed in the coil. The iron bar becomes a magnet. The combination of coil and iron is called an electromagnet, which has N and S poles. The strength of an electromagnet is proportional to the number of turns in the coil, to the strength of the current, and depends upon the material used for the core. For the same core material it is proportional to the ampere turns. An electromagnet is used in the operation of the electric bell, telegraph, telephone, lifting crane, motor, generator, and many electrical control devices.

Electrolysis: An electric current is used to decompose substances which will dissociate into ions in solution. 

Decomposition of water. An electric current decomposes water into hydrogen and oxygen.

Electroplating. An electric current is used to plate one metal on another metal. The object to be plated is connected to the cathode or negative terminal. The anode is composed of the metal which is to be plated on the cathode. The solution contains the plating metal. 

Water + sulfuric acid Copper sulfate (CuSO4) solution

Storage cell. The lead storage cell is composed of plates of lead and a dilute solution of sulfuric acid. The storage cell is charged by passing a direct current (d.c.) through the cell. The current causes a chemical change in the plates and in the solution. 

When charged the lead storage cell acts in a manner similar to that of any other cell. The lead storage cell has a very low internal resistance and an E.M.F. of approximately 2 volts. The specific gravity of the solution in the cell when charged is approximately 1.3; when discharged it is about 1.12. 303

Unit 9. Electromagnetism-Induced Currents Induced voltage and current: An electromotive force is induced when a magnetic field is moved across a conductor. The quantity of the electromotive force induced depends on the speed with which the lines of force are cut. The field set up by the induced current in the coil opposes the field of the inducing magnet (Lenz's law). are fewer turns in the secondary than in the primary. Hence, the voltage decreases and the current increases.

Primary coil

The generator. The essential parts of a generator are the coil of wire, the magnetic field, the brushes, and the slip rings or commutator. The induced electromotive force is proportional to the number of turns of wire in the coil, the strength of the magnetic field, and the speed with which the coil is rotated. Magnets or electromagnets can be used for the field. A generator produces an alternating current (A.C.). The alternating current can be converted to a direct current by the use of a commutator. A generator converts mechanical energy into electrical energy.

Induced current from D. C. An induced current can be produced by using a pulsating, direct current. The direct current is passed through a primary coil. The primary coil is placed inside a secondary coil. An electromotive force is induced in the secondary coil by making and breaking the circuit, by moving an iron core in the primary coil, or by moving either coil. An induced current will flow in the secondary circuit whenever there is a change in the strength of the current in the primary circuit. 

The induction coil. The induction coil is used to produce a high electromotive force to make possible an electrical discharge. The induction coil is composed of an iron core, a primary coil of few turns, a secondary coil of many turns, and a circuit breaker. A condenser is sometimes connected in the primary circuit to increase the size of the spark discharge. An induction coil is used to produce the spark across the terminals of the spark plug in the gas engine.

The transformer. In a transformer, the alternating current in the primary coil induces a current in the secondary coil. The current induced is increased by placing an iron core in the primary coil. 

In a step-up transformer the voltage is increased and the amperage is decreased by having many more turns of wire in the secondary than in the primary. Because the field of an alternating current is constantly changing, it is not necessary to have a circuit breaker. In a step-down transformer there Secondary coil- The telephone. In a telephone, messages are sent over wires by the use of induced currents. In the primary circuit the voice waves cause the plate in the transmitter to vibrate. These vibrations cause a motion of the carbon particles. The vibration of the carbon particles varies the resistance of the primary circuit and also the strength of the current in the circuit. A changing current in the primary sets up an induced current in the secondary circuit. 

In the receiver the permanent magnet is alternately strengthened and weakened by the changes of polarity in the poles of the electromagnet. This changing magnetic strength causes the iron diaphragm in the receiver to vibrate. The vibrations of the diaphragm in the receiver correspond to the vibrations of the sound waves entering the transmitter.

Lenz's law in A.C. In a coil, the alternating current sets up a magnetic field which opposes the flow of the current through the coil. 

Kinds of current: Graphic representations of different kinds of current: (1) direct current from a cell, (2) alternating current, (3) rectified current by commutator, (4) rectified current by vacuum tube. (1) (2) (3) (4) 304

The motor: An electric motor converts electrical energy into mechanical energy. The essential parts of a D.C. motor are the armature, the magnetic field, the brushes, and the commutator. The armature of the motor is caused to rotate by the force between the magnetic fields of the armature and the field magnet.

A permanent magnet or an electromagnet can be used for the field magnet. A motor produces a back 

E.M.F. as soon as its armature begins to rotate.

Unit 10. Sound 

Production of sound: Sounds are produced by set ting matter in vibration. Example: A violin string makes no sound unless it is moving. 

Wave motion: Sounds are transmitted through matter by longitudinal waves. The particles of the medium transmitting a sound vibrate in the line of the direction in which the sound travels. Sound waves will not pass through a vacuum. Wave length

Sound reflection is used in measuring the depth of water or the altitude of an airplane. 

Pitch: Pitch is determined by the rate of vibration of sound. The ear can hear rates of vibration as low as 20 per sec. and as high as 20,000 per sec. 

Middle C has a vibration rate of 256 per sec. 

The wave equation: The rate of vibration is equal to the velocity of the sound waves divided by the wave length: or 

Number of vibra tions per sec.

Speed of sound: Sound waves travel through air at a speed of 1089 ft. per sec., or 331.7 m per sec., at 0°C. Sound waves travel through water about 

4 times as fast as in air, and in iron about 15 times as fast as in air. The speed of sound is increased by approximately 60 cm per sec. if the temperature rises by 1 centigrade degree. 343.7 m or 1130 ft. in one sec. at 20° C 

Reflection of sound: Sound waves are reflected when they strike surfaces. The sounds heard by reflection are called echoes. The reflecting surface must be at least 55 ft. from the source of sound. Wall- -55 ft. or more Boat/ Water

Sound- Direct Waves Echo Reflected 

Loudness: The loudness of a sound increases if the amplitude of the wave increases. Sound amplifiers make sounds louder by increasing the amplitudes of the waves. A sound travels a greater distance if the amplitude of the waves is increased. The pitch of a sound is not changed by a change in amplitude. Amplitude:

АААА Quality: The quality of a sound is determined by a combination of different frequencies. If the combination of different frequencies is pleasing to hear, the sound is said to be harmonious; if not pleasing, a discord is produced.

Music and noises: Musical sounds have a uniform rate of vibration. Noises have irregular rates of vibration. 

Frequency of a wire. The frequency of a wire is inversely proportional to its length, directly proportional to the square root of the tension, and inversely proportional to the square root of the mass per unit length. A short wire has a higher frequency 305

than a long wire of the same tension, diameter, and material: n₁/n₂ =/2/11. If the length of a wire is doubled, its frequency is half as great. The greater 'the tension on a wire the higher its frequency: ni T. If the tension is made four times as great, nVT 2 the frequency is twice as great.

A wire of large diameter has a lower frequency than a small wire of the same material, tension, and length. The mass per unit length is proportional to of the diameter: m2 ni d2. ոլ n2 Vmi or n2 If dı the square the diameter of a wire is doubled, its frequency is half as great.

D vibrates twice as fast as C. no √10 = 1/2 Overtones in a wire. A wire can be made to vibrate as a whole and in segments at the same time. If a wire vibrates in one segment, the lowest or fundamental tone is produced. If a wire vibrates in two segments, the rate of vibration is twice as great as the fundamental, and the first overtone is produced. If a wire vibrates in three segments, the second overtone is produced. 

If two sound waves meet in the same phase, the sound becomes loud. If they meet in opposite phase, the sound is not so loud; if they meet in opposite phase and the amplitudes are equal, silence results. AAAAAAAÁ

Resonance in a pipe: Resonance is produced in a pipe by the combination of direct and reflected waves. The best resonant length for a pipe closed at one end is 4 of the wave length of the sound produced. Succeeding points of resonance are 3/4 and 5/4 of the wave length. Only alternate overtones can be produced in a closed pipe. The best resonant length for a pipe open at both ends is 1/2 of the wave length of the sound produced. All succeeding overtones can be produced in the open pipe.

Six beats are produced each second when sounds of frequencies 250 and 256 vibrations per sec. are produced together. 5/315/8

Musical instruments: Musical instruments are designed to make possible sounds of a wide range of frequencies and combinations of frequencies. which are pleasing to hear. 

Stringed instruments. Stringed instruments have wires of different lengths, diameters, materials, and tensions. Resonance is produced by the use of sounding boards or boxes. 

Wind instruments. Wind instruments have pipes of different lengths. Some pipes are closed at one end; others are open at both ends. A very large number of pipes is needed for a good pipe organ. 

Percussion instruments. In percussion instruments, sounds are produced by vibrating membranes or discs. 306

Unit 11. Light 

Sources of light: Our main source of light is the sun. Light is produced artificially by heating materials to incandescence, such as particles of carbon, filaments, and gases. 

Heated particles

Tungsten 

Shadows. A shadow is formed when an opaque. object is placed in the path of rays of light, since the rays do not bend around its edges. 

Reflection of light: A ray of light is reflected from a surface with the angle of incidence equal to the angle of reflection. Smooth surfaces make the best reflectors. Diffusion is the reflection of light from a rough surface.

Carbon Normal Incident. ray Reflected ray

Angle of incidence 

-Angle of reflection 

Wave motion: Light is transmitted by transverse wave motion through a vacuum or through matter.. 

The particles transmitting the light vibrate at right angles to the direction in which the light travels. 

Speed of light: Light travels at a speed of 186,300 mi. per sec., or 299,800 km per sec., in a vacuum.

The wave equation. The frequency is equal to the velocity of light waves divided by the wave length. n = 

Luminous and illuminated objects: An object which gives off its own light is said to be luminous.

An object is illuminated if it is seen by reflected light. 

Intensity of light: Intensity of illumination decreases as the square of the distance from its source. 

The unit of intensity of light is the candle-power. 

The foot-candle is the intensity of illumination received on a point at a distance of 1 ft. from a source of one candle-power. The lumen is the quantity of light received on an area of 1 ft.2 all of which is at a distance of 1 ft. from a source of 1 candle-power. 1 ft.2 Lumen 1 ft. candle ft. candle 

Smooth surface regular reflection 

Rough surface diffused reflection 

Images formed by mirrors. Plane and curved mirrors will form images. A plane mirror forms a virtual image of the same size as the object, and as far behind the mirror as the object is in front of the mirror. Parallel rays of light striking a concave mirror are reflected to cross one another at a point called the principal focus. Rays of light passing through the center of curvature of the mirror and striking the mirror are reflected back through the center of curvature. A real image is formed with a concave mirror if the object is placed outside the principal focus. A virtual image is formed if the object is placed inside the principal focus. A virtual image only can be formed with a convex mirror.

Refraction of light: Rays of light are refracted when they pass from one medium into another medium of different density. Light travels more slowly in glass and water than in air. The ratio of the speed of light in air or in a vacuum to its speed in glass is the index of refraction of glass.

In astigmatism not all parts of the iris focus light rays in the same plane. The image is blurred. Astigmatism is corrected by using glasses with lenses shaped to correct the defect.

Images formed by lenses. Each surface of a lens is an arc of a circle. A convex lens brings parallel rays of light together at the principal focus. 

A convex lens forms a real image when the object is placed outside the principal focus. A virtual image is formed when the object is placed inside the principal focus. Virtual images only are formed with concave lenses.

The microscope. In a microscope a small object is magnified. The objective lens in a compound microscope forms a real inverted image of the object at the top of the barrel. The object is outside the principal focus of the objective lens. The eyepiece lens magnifies the real image. The magnified image is a virtual image because the lens is placed nearer the real image than its focal length. The focal length of the average eye is 10 in. or 25 cm. Color: Different colors are produced by light of different wave lengths. 

Dispersion of light. Dispersion is the separation of a beam of light into its different colors by the use of a prism. Different colors have different wave lengths. The short waves are bent more than the long waves. Sunlight is composed of the colors seen in the rainbow or soap film. The colors are represented by the letters vi bgyor. The band of colors formed by a prism is called the spectrum. Sunlight Yellow clothes Bluing

White clothes 

Mixing pigments. The three primary pigments are peacock blue, crimson, and green. The primary pigments are the complements of the primary colors. 

Pigments subtract from one another when mixed. 

Blue and yellow paints when mixed produce a green paint. The three primary pigments when mixed appear to be black. All the colors are absorbed. Red

Peacock blue Red

Orange Yellow Green Bluish-violet 

Crimson 2 Yellow Green -Blue Indigo Violet Mixing colors. The three primary colors are red, green, and bluish-violet. Any two colors, which combine to form white, are complementary colors. 

Blue and yellow are, complementary colors. The three primary colors can be combined to form the other colors. Colors add to one another when mixed. 

Mixing Light Mixing Pigments 

Spectra: Continuous spectra are produced by incandescent solids, liquids and dense gases. Discontinuous, or bright line, spectra are produced by luminous gases or vapors. Absorption or dark line, spectra are produced when a luminous vapor absorbs light waves of the same wave lengths as the vapor can emit. 

Unit 12. Electronics; Radiation 

Production of radio waves: Radio waves are produced by oscillations in an electrical circuit. These oscillations may be damped or undamped. The usual type of oscillations are undamped. The frequency of oscillation, and hence, of the radio waves, is determined by the inductance and capacitance of the circuit. Undamped oscillations produce carrier waves of constant amplitude. Sound waves striking the microphone cause modulated waves of varying amplitude. The frequency of the radio waves from any one station does not change. 

Radio transmission: Radio waves carry energy with the velocity of light. Radio waves are very long in comparison with light waves. The length varies from approximately 1 cm to several hundred meters. 

The fundamental wave equation, v=nl, holds here as well as in all types of wave motion. 

Radio reception: Radio messages are received and amplified by vacuum tubes. A detector separates the audio frequency from the modulated waves. Detection and amplification with vacuum tubes are possible because electrons flow from the hot filament Electron mw m m ww Vacuum tube Radio waves Grid leak Electron motion 40 flow Sound reproducer Tuning condenser

B battery 

Oscillations 

A battery 40 10 309

to the cold plate. The grid of a vacuum tube provides a sensitive control for the plate current. The volume of sound is increased by using more tubes for amplification. The circuits in the receiving set must be tuned to the same frequency as that of the transmitting set. Tuning in the receiver is usually accomplished by changing the capacitance of the grid circuit. 

The photo-electric cell: In a photo-electric cell, or "electric eye", electrons are emitted by a particular kind of material in a vacuum tube when light falls upon it. The amount of current through the tube varies with the amount of light falling upon the tube. 

X rays: X rays are produced by causing high-speed electrons to bombard a target in a vacuum tube. 

These waves are very short in comparison with light waves. X rays are very penetrating, are very useful in killing diseased tissue, and in enabling the physician to detect broken bones as well as evidences of disease. They also serve many practical uses in industry. ееее Tungsten target mmm mot ​Light

Electrons wwwww X rays 

Television: Television makes use of a vacuum tube in which a stream of electrons fall upon a lightsensitive material on which is focused the image to be broadcast. The electron beam is made to scan the image by use of electric and magnetic fields. In the receiving set, a similar electron beam falls upon a fluorescent screen in a vacuum tube, thus reproducing the image. 

Infra-red and ultra-violet rays: Waves that are just longer than those of the visible spectrum carry energy, which becomes heat upon falling upon matter. This region of the spectrum is known as the infra-red. The ultra-violet is the part of the spectrum in which the waves are just shorter than those of the visible spectrum. Energy in these waves is effective in tanning the skin, producing a change in a photographic film, and in curing certain diseases. 

Cathode rays: Cathode rays are electrons driven from an electrode in a vacuum tube by means of high voltage. Their direction can be changed by electric and magnetic fields.

Cathode Radium: The nucleii of radium atoms, and the atoms of similar elements, spontaneously emit alpha particles, beta particles, and gamma rays. The alpha particles are positively charged particles with Beta particles Gamma rays Alpha particles ellee rays Vacuum Cathode Anode a mass four times that of the hydrogen atom. The beta particles are electrons. The gamma rays are radiation of very short wave length, and are very penetrating. They are found to be very useful in killing diseased tissue. 310
